TO THE MOSS ON A FRIEND'S GRAVE. Although the winter stern and drear • Has stripped the trees, and branches bare Against the sky stand cold and cleat As if in mute, appealing prayer, This fragile moss, so fresh and green, Defies the storm and tempest wild, And lifts itahead, the,snow between, As if again the summer smiled. • The flowers th tt bloamed have faded all, Of their bright bqauty nought remains, .t , Hushed_is each feathered songster's call,. And all around Death's silence reigns. • Yet does this fresh and springing moss Arise to cheer the mournful scene; A type of life, to bridge across The gulf 'twixt now, and what has been. A speaking, sign of newer life -1 It crowns the grave where deaiones sleep At rekfrom Earth's kind din and strife- -. • And slie May read its meaning deep. A symbol of the radiant clime Wbere none but holy feet have trod; Where meet we, as in olden time, Beloved ones whd sleep in God. • , J. H. G., Hull.

" CAPITAL CHAT."--

Mr. Grant Allen, who in recent review articles has shown a tendency towards certain Socialistic doctrines, has applied to become of „the Fabian Society.

It is not unlikely, that the Rev. G. S. Heaney will write a little book giving expression to his reasons for seceding to the State Church. He has refused offers to write articles on. the subject front several editors.

Mr. Gladstone makes it a rule never to travel on Sunday. He consequently returned from DolliB Hill on Saturday night in order to hear the Bishop of Peterborough at St. Margaret's, Westmidster, on Sunday morning.

. This rule of Mr. Gladstone' against Sunday travelling (according to the "Manchester Guardian' correspondent) placed him in a rather inconvenient position when at one time the Queen took to invit- ing him to dine and sleep at Windsor on Saturday night, and did not extend ker invitation over Sun- day. When this occurred, Mr. Gladstone used to remove himself on Sunday from the Castle to the Deanery,and remain till Monday as the guest of Dean Wellesley. This came to the Queen's ears, and a r more considerate arrangement was adopted.

Mr. Louis J. Jennings, M.P., has been at work again unearthing official abuses. Ile has prepared an elaborate case against the War Office, showing some startling instances of wasteful expenditure. Mr. Jennings gives poor Mr. Ash mead Bartlett a hard time of it.

There is something for the Archbishop of Canter- bury and the "Church Times" to prayerful ly con- sider. It is stated that of the three thousand Mormons who recently met in convention in New Zealand no less than two thousand were ex-mem- bers of the Church of England.

It may be worth while mentioning that Mdme. Patti's fee for Saturday afternoon's concert was /SOO ; not a bad afternoon's work. Mr. Percy Harrison has been fortunate enough to obtain Mdme. Patti's services for two—years—for certain concerts in London, and a series in the provinces. Lucky Mr. Harrison.

A knowledge of the Welsh langnage is evidently not possessed by the Ecclesiastical Commissioners, for on a vicar in the Principality sending up in the usual way the name of a new church- in his parish, 1 the Commissioners' wrote back to ask to what saint the edifice had been dedicated, _not suspecting that the name forwarded them was the vernacular for the Virgin Mary. ,

Dr. John Mackintosh, author of the " History of Scotland" which Mr. Fisher Unwin is about ter publish, is a secondhand bookseller of Aberdeen. He is a regular dryasdust, and pores over the books. His customers used to wonder what he was always doing. As it turned out be was writing his excel- lent History of Civilization in Scotland," for which the University of his native town conferred on him the degree of LL.D.

Lady Colin Campbell. who writes the articles "A Woman's Walks," which appear every week in the World over the signature "Vera T. Tsaritsyn," is said to be "one of the most efficient, reliable, and th °roughly business-like journalists (men or women) in England." Lady Colin is also "a most careful book-reviewer, a very fair art critic, and a first-rate descriptive writer.' Our authority, Woman, adds that" she can use d typewriter herself."

Mr. Labouchere has discovered a new instance of the Queen's economy. He learns that whenever her Majesty sends servants, horses, carriages, and heavy luggage by sea to Scotland or elsewhere, all the expenses are paid by the Admiralty, on the ground that George IV. used to send these ifrpedimenta to Scotland by sea, and that if the Queen prefers to send hers by land she is entitled to "free delivery," as she might have sent them free by sea.

Further particulars are to hand concerning the triumphs won in Melbourne by Sir Charles and Lady Halle. Immense assemblages filled the auditorium of the Town Hall on each occasion of their appear- ance, and their perfermances created, a well-nigh unprecedented amount of enthusiasm. Sir Charles Mall's interpretation of Beethoven's sonatas evoked special eulogy, one example being forthcoming at each concert; while the Australian critics appear to have exhausted all their superlatives on Lady Hanes playing of her briefer solos.

The biggest thing in the,way of the organisation of female labour that has yet been attempted is now being pushed forward by the Melbourne Trades Hall Council. It is nothing less than a monster union of servants, barmaids, and waitresses. The Melbourne Council have organised every male trade in the Victorian metropolis, and have succeeded in getting. one large branch of female labor—the tailoresses—tp organise. The tailoresses have built an assembly room of their own on the Trades Hall reserve, and hold their weekly meetings with business-like order and regularity.

Stanley's "In Darkest Africa" is already an assured success in the most emphatic—that is, in the financial—sense of the term. Over and above the immense number of copies sent out on baturday and Monday last, the publishers, it is stated, are now selling 500 additional copies a day. This for a two-guinea book, and one, moreover, which was boldly subscribed for by the" trade" to begin with, is in itself a remarkable proof of the keen and wide- spread interest which everywhere prevails to read the book itseltand not to be content as usual with a mere "review " of it.

-- Emin Pasha has just sent an interesting letter to Mr. F. A. Brockhaus, the Gdrman publisher, in re- gard to overtures for an account of his experiences in Equatoria. He states that he has already received "ten different invitations to book-writing from Germany alone," but that, for the present at all events, he must decline the task. At the same time he promises that if he does find leisure on his new expedition to do any literary work the manuscript shall at once be forwarded to Mr. Brockhaus. Emin asks that his Germancorrespondent should at once send him the first available copy of Stanley's work.

Mr. James A. Duncan, the Liberal member for Barrow, is connected with the town by reason of the fact that his father, the late Mr. David Duncan, was its tirst member. Mr. Duncan is thirty-two. He was educated at Amersham Hall School and at Trinity College, Cambridge, of which he is an and L.L.B. He is a member of the Inner Temple, and is on the Northern Circuit. Mr. Duncan, Who is an active member of the Eighty Club, is an effective, speaker, and soon made himself pepular with the people of Barrow. Mr. Stephen Williamson, the member for the Kilmarnock Burghs, is Mr. Dun- can's uncle.

Freedom of divorce seems to have become a 1 plank of the new party. It is now recommended 1 59 the right remedy for desertion ; and a Bill em- bodying this principle has been introduced to Par- liament by Mr. Hunter, Mr. Asquith, Air. Cobb, Mr. Venwick, and Mr. Herbert Gardner. Its chief clause does away with the necessity for the plea of cruelty in the petition of a wife, and would give husband and wife equally a claim to divorce' if guilty of desertion for four years and np*ards without reasonable excuse. The term of • reasonable ex- cuse "is left very vague. The measure is undoubt edly a sign of the times.

-- Mr. Crawford, the member for Durham, is dead. He was one of the Radical working-class members who were returned for the first time in 1885. He was a Northumberland miner, was born in Northum- berland, educated in Northumberland, and worked in a Northumbrian pit. He became a union leader some years ago, and at last became almost the chief agent of the Durham Miners' Association. For his services he was named, after the Reform Bill of 1885, to represent the Durham miners. When Mr. Gladstone declared in favour of Home Rule, Mr. Crawford at once ranged himself on the side of Ins leader, and that he had acted according to the opinion of his constituents was made clear by his return without opposition in 1880.

The conference between the Leeds Corporation Gas Committee and the representatives of the stokers was lesumed on Thursday, and a report of the prooceedings was made to a special meeting of the Town Council, at which steps were taken which, it is confidently expected will lead to a speedy settlement of the dispute. The town was again on Thursday night in darkness, but there was no renewal of the disturbances. - -----

A DRINK or Max.—The tempting form of "milk shake" is compounded with some aerated or sparkling water or soda waters. To make pure milk as sold in towns more easily digestible than it is to some persons, all that is necessary, says a medical adviser, is to shake it up well together, and to sip it slowly. Shake the milk violently for a few minutes in a covered goblet, and then drink it immediately (but not hastily) before it has time to separate aeain. Alter standing some hours, as all city milk has done on its way to town, the perfectly homogenous article that came from the cow is separated,

first by the rising of the cream from the osseous or cheesy milk, and later by the tendency to divide into whey and curd. Although this latest process is percep- tible only in souring milk, the fluid undergoes such con- stant changes that it is Dr. Morgan's opinion it should always be shaken before drinking it, except it is taken quite warm from the cow. TEACH THE Gram TO SWIM. —Girls should more gene- rally be taught the delightful amusement of swimming. To jump into the water and breast the waves is as ex- hilarating to the spirits as hunting and jumping over a good fence.. It is also a glorious exercise—one of the best that can be taken. It expands the chest; it promotes digestion; it des-lopes the muscles bringing into action some Muscles which are seldom brought into play*; it strengthens and braces the whole frame, and thus makes the swimmer resist the liabilities of catching cold. Swimming gives a lady courage, energy, and sell-reliance; it may be the means of saying human life—this alone is a great recommenda- tion. Swimming is best taught to a girl in a large • swimming bath, and afterward it may be indulged in in the sea. QUICK WORK TN A LAW COUET.—Mr. Serj cant Robin- son says that Common Serjeant Mirehouse got through his cases with extraordinary rapidity, yet was a man of strict integrity, determined to do justice to all. "I have known Mirehouse," he says, "more than once sentence a man to seven years transportation at the end of as many minute's from the commencement of the trial." This was the common form with him. A prisoner might be indicted for stealing a purse from a woman in the street. The Common Serjeant to the prosecutrix, after getting her name and address: "Were you in such a street on such a day ?" "Yes, my lord." "Dad you a purse in your hand?" "Yes, my lord." "Policeman, produce the purse. Is that your purse ?" "Yes, my lord." "Did you see the prisoner?" "Yes, my lord:" " What did he do?" "He snatched the purse from me, and ran away." Mirehouse to the prisoner: " Prisoner, do you Wish to ask the witness anything?" "No, my lord." "Policeman, did you apprehend the prisoner?" yes, my lord, and found this purse upon him." "Did he say anything ?" "He said he picked it up in the street." "Prisoner, do you wish to say anything to the jury ?" "No, my lord, except what I told the police- man is true." V:firehouse "Well, gentlemen of the jury, if you believe the evidence, you will say that the pri- soner is guilty. Ile says lie picked up the purse in the street. I have walked the streets of London for many years now, and I have never been lucky enough tomick up a purse; perhaps some of you have. Consider your verdict." 'The verdict would be speedily given, and the Common Serjeant, without using a single superfluous word, would pass sentence. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 5,489.0.

HOW TO WIN ELECTIONS.

(BY P. M. CAMPBELL, LIBERAL AGENT TOR THE NEWTON DIVISION). (Concluded.) It •is a matter of course that any suggested change in the system, designed to supply present deficiencies and to comply with the arduous essen- tials- here laid down, must differ in principle very widely. from anything hitherto tried among us. That is the fault of, the needs of the case, not of those who propose remedies. The gist of any change, in the system should be in the direction of the wide development of individual responsibility. At present some 300 or 500 persons are expected to look more or less vaguely after the whole body of the local Liberals, and the local Liberal policy. For this should be substituted a system, the basis of which should be that every individual should be the special care of some other individual nember of the party. The principle is simplicity itself. The application is not very difficult, and need, not introduce any great revolu- tion into the present form of organisation. But it is the spirit that must be entirely changed. The size of the Newton Division -may be taken as the average size ,of single-member constituencies, or taken roughly, say at 10,000. In London the 'actual average may be placed at 8,000, with a Tory pro- portion of votes judged by the polling, of about 19 to 17. In the English boroughs, ttie average is about 8,500 and the balance of party votes about equal ; and in the counties the average is 'about 10,000, and the Liberal votes and the proportion of a little more than three to.two. This is only a rough estimate, gathered from tho figures published by "The Pall Mall Gazette." As the difficulties of orgadisation are almost proportionate to the size of the electorate, it will be better to err in too large than too small an estimate. Let us take therefore our own constituency, 'or one with an electorate.of. 10,000 in which there are, or should be, 6,000 Liber- als." The principal of the proposed organisatiort is to divide the whole party into small groups, each under the care of an active member of the partk, , and to sub-divide the leaders of these grouprinto other groups, and so render them easily susceptible , to influence.

THE STARTING-POINT of the organisation should be the division of the rank and file into small groups. The number of each group should not exceed twelve, and thus in a perfected organization there would be about 500 of them. Each group Should be ' under the care of a leader elected from and by the members of id them- selves. The leader should be responsible for the bealthy political condition of the members. He would keep himself informed of their opinions, see that they had proper knowledge of political quepticries ; and 'undertake the practical work of registration, canvassing, and polling. ,In return they should look to him for any information on political affairs 'that they needed, and he should be their mouthpiece in the affairs of the party. The political ties between them should be made as close as possible. All this would be easily practicable where the leader and group together did not exceed twelve in number.f These 500 group leaders should form a Genera Council, and would answer roughly in form to the 300 or 500 of the present system. But the similarity would bern form onlyets the definite personal work and responsibility would 'change the character of the duties to be done, and the spirit in which they would be done. Experience has shown, however, that 500 workers in a constituency are not to be found sufficiently zealous to carry in their work without bode -inducement and supervision. These 500 group leaders or whatever else you may name them, would be themselves divided into groups 'of ten. And we suggest that locality would be found the most workable basis of any plan of division. Each of these groups should elect its leader, who should be its representative in a council of PO, answering again roughly in form to the Executive Committee of the present system. As in the case of the group of the rank and file, so each member of this s'econd council so chosen should be responsible for keeping the other nine members up to their work, receiving from them reports, offering them such form of inducement as the constituency and their circumstances might afford, suggesting to them the course of their work, and generally super- vising them. Experience, again, comes in to show that even executive committees can doze, and that the real work of management is done by a much smaller body (in this division notably by less than one tenth of the whole number). These fifty should therefore be again grouped, and each ten should elect two members to a control council of ten, on whom the main directions of the work of the local party would fall. In addition to the arrangement of the party matters, and to their work as members of the Councils of 50 and 500 each, two members should be required to keep their eight electors up to their duties. In such a system, when phrfected, there would be two advantages of paramount im- portance; there-would be close connected and inti- mate relations between the numbers of the whole jitney from the top to the bottom,. and each man would have his work and his responsibility, neither being beyond his capacity. ANNUAL ELECTIONS, ' The election ot every mettiber of the association should take place annually some time in winter, since that is the time at which political feeling is most easily aroused. Had we been doing six months ago what we are doing now, we should have been all the better for it. Annual elections would be necessary to ensure that thewhole party was in touch, and to keep alive political ,interest. The best time for them would probably be in the month of January, before the meeting of Parlise ment. The method of election should, be by ballot- card, and not at meetings. It is not, of course, con- tended that such an organization, can be created immediately, or without much hard: work. But it is contended, in the first place. that it is practicable; , and in the second,that the present oandidature and prospects of the party are such as to offer the neces- sary time that is wanted for the work. The first difficulties would be considerable., and the perfect- ing could only be carried out after some time. The present obstacles would arise• mainly from the former errors., They are chiefly concerned with our unfortunate uncertainty as to who are and who are not Liberals. This would touch. the first stop— namely, the division of the party into groups. That we cannot do all that *e would iseao,reason, how- ever, why we should not do all that we can: ande even as matters are, we can do a great deal. If on. of our supposititious 10,000 we cannot get 6,000 'Liberals, We should begin with 44000, or 3,000, oe 2,000, begin anywhere that we can find a starting point. It weuld be, moreover, probably impossible. at first to linve the gioup leaders- elected. They would have to be either appointeddrom with in the party, or solicited to, volunteer. The full number of 500 could, however, and should„ be got ' together, ' and they should be selected, as far aspossible;froda the same social ranks aothOse who are believedfto constitute the bulk, of the party.. The getter* together of ehe 500 'ought not to.be difficult, if tbe present association were utilized for the purpose, together with Liberals Clubs, where they exist. Every means should be adopted to stir up local enthusiasm, and, what is so rarely done, advantage should be promptly taken of, this when aroused. Directly the 500 were forthcoming, the minor councils might at once be formed, and personal work and responsibility forthwith developed. There would be bat poor heart in the work if within twelve months the limits of the party -were not sufficiently ascertained to- admit of the method ; of election being possible among the first:groups. Obviously the work of each,of the 500 during that year would largely be the development of. his par- ticular group. Nixing as fresey and frevently possible with these in hieeharge, each group leaden. could hardly fail to discoVer their political lean- ings, and so be. able to enrol them as, friends or report them es antagonists. In two years, or at the outside three years,the system sho,ild be firmly' established. Such an organization as we lave• sketched would be, in the first place, 11110ROUGZGX DRMOCRATra,

and calculated to gain the confidence of all classes of the party. It would ensure the amplest re- presentation of all kinds and degrses of opinion, and would yet be free from the taint of wire-pul- ling in its ordinary and unpleasant sensee. The opinion when expressed would be the opinion of all, and not of a few. They would be the result of the collected thought of the whole party. It would be, moreover, a strong feature, that. the electors would be led by the men of their own class, whom they have elected to lead them. Each member of the MO would thus most thoroughly understand the wishes and desires of those whom he represented, and would, as it were, breathe the , same political air with them. The smallness of the groups, their number, and the frequency of election would hinder the fostering of those unpleasant ele- ments of cliquism and jealousy which have wrought ' so much distrust. DEATH OE MR. W. CRAWFORD, M.P.

The Press. Association's Durham correspondent tele- graphs that Mr. Wm. Crawford, member for Mid-Dur- ham, died at his residence, North-roads Durham, at six o'clock on Tuegday mwning, somewhat unexpectedly. Mr. Crawford had been under medical treatment for a considerable time, but was able to attend to his Parlia- mentary and other business, and was present during a portion of a meeting at the Coal Trade Hall, Newcastle, on Monday. Fatal symptoms set in at five o'clock on Tuesday morning.' Mr. Crawford was returned as the Liberal member for Mid-Durham at the general election of 1835 by 5,799 votes, against the Hon. A. V. Tempest (C), who polled 3,245. The aeceased member accepted the Home Rule programme, and was re-elected in 1888 wbhout opposition.

The pro- cess would be simple, rapid, direct, effective, and cheap. The opinion of the party could also be gathered with the greatest possible swiftness on any particular measure or proposal, whether of the Government or Opposition. The importance of this cannot be over-estimated. Hitherto we have had to rely upon close or public meetings. The power under the proposed system would be infinitely greater and more valuable. would be a work of Much greater ease than at pre- sent. Instead of the indiscriminate sprinkling and scattering of a few drops of political information in an amateur and entirely haphazard manner, the waters from the chief fountains would be carried by proper channels throughout the whole party. The practical work of registration would be lightened to a vast extent. The group leaders would have charge of their immediate groups, and any change of residence among the members would be at once known and noted. From the members themselves would be ' 'gained easily enough the knowledge of new comers and the task of the agent would be minimised. The importance of this con- oideration can only be eotimated when we bear ,in mind that the residence qualification must soon be greatly reduced, and unless some thoroughly eci- ent and popular. machinery is in existence, the agents will be powerless to cope With the difficul- ties caused by the removals of small householders. •Again, in simplifying proceeding, at elections the value of such a system would be more apparent than even in registration work. In view of the reduc- tion of election expenses, prior tO their being thrown upon the rates, this is a matter of the first , consequence. It is for the Liberals to shoW how cheaply elections can on the average be conducted. This is wanted as a guide for future legislation. A thoroughly democratic organisation is the surest preliminary—indeed, the great essential of cheap- ness. In canvassing the merits of such an organisa- tion would also be conspicuous. Instead of a number of volunteers, whose commendable enthu- siasm is too frequently counter-balanced by their unfortunate inexperience, there would be a large band of men, each knowing his work and trained by former experience, instantly available: In poll- ing the electors, the work would agate) be so sub- divided as to be extremely simple. Could it be possible that lie— Layton—was her companion? Such a suggestion made his blood boll, and he started to his feet as if to punish the couple who had thus played him false. It was only during the afternoon of the day just gone that he had had an assurance of affection from Lucy, as he imagined it 'Lebo. And now he was despised and scorned for a rival of a few weeks' standing. Ho swore to be revenged. George Collier was a determined man, yet not altogether a self-reliant one in emergency. He was quick to execute and rapid in his judgments, but they were not always reliable. He was honest and brave' and had made every effort to win Lucy, though, perhaps, making too sure of her. We may take a retrospective glance and relate the circum- stances wh7ch he fancied gave him an undoubted claim upon Lucy's affection. . . . Ilt. was well !known in Bowdale. when the railway came into the district, and he got employment. After a time George was sent up to the signals and enjoyed his new position. He may well have done so, physically, for the views spread out before him was extensive and beautiful. .0n the right an arm of the sea, the crisp waves beating on the curving beach, round which embanked the new-laid railway ran; beyond a river pouring through the wooded land into the bay, and purple hills which dated from an epoch thou- sands of years before, ere ice and snow had wrapped them in their chilling garments, and the land had been locked in wintry grasp for ages. - A fair prospect to look npou in summer, 'and George Collier gazed upon it with much pleasureable feeling. He was still occupied:in the yiew, watching the setting sun slowly descending, and the sea all aflame to welcome it, when across the fiery disc he perceived a boat, rowed by a Tonna-nisi alono

She apProaclied the land ripaivlhere the river swirled and tossed the flowing tide. The rain up amongst the purple hills had swollen the stream, and sent the waters from the hidden lake rushing to the sea in freedom. But the,salt water dick not like this freshfinroad on its wide domain, and it resisted stoutly, fighting inch by inch,fassisted by the wind which got up to struggle with the river. As Collier gazed, his attention was drawn to the boat, which had now by mismanagement got into'the race of the tide, and was 'leaping in a dangerous fashion in the short sea that rose around it. He did not like the look of things at all. The girl who was rowing seemed quite unable to manage the boat, and there was no one,lieatto help or shout for it. the line. The young signalman glanced at the clock behie d him. He knew the mineral train was &se to shunt close by, and make room for a passenger train tc London. He gave the beat " all clear" and passed it on and looked out again. The boat was still teased about in the eddy. The girl could make but little way.. The sun had dipped —an angry light came across the tumbling water. A wave approached and almost tarried the boat over. George Collier could not stand it any longer. With- out a moment for reflection he rushed down the lofty steps, and hurried out along the Thee to gain the beach and save the boat if possible. The line was single than, andllaid beside the sea. Collier would have had no difficulty in reaching the water side but he kept the line and hastened' on. He had almost reached the boat when it upset,. and falling over in the rush of fighting waves it tilled and disappeared. Along deep whistle sounded the- requiem of the hapless occupant. Was he too late ?. George Collier heard the whistlis, and his heart stood still with sheer terror. He suddenly recollected that the passenger train was due at die cross-over, and the line being single, the mineral waggons would have to be shunted into the siding to-anake way for the fast train. No wonder his bloc& froze in his veins. The two Veins would soon be rushing to their destruction, and no earthly power could neer arrest their progress. It was true that the passenger train would slacken speed, when the driver caught sight el the danger signal, bat the mineral train would come on withont. a cheek and dash into the stationary vehicles.. George Collier felt as a murderer might be sup- posed to feel, and yet he was bent upon saving life at that vary' moment. These thoughts passed through his brain in a few- seconds as he was hastening to rescuethe =for- t:mate women in the water. The boat was floatine- Bottom upwards; a piece of a dress was vis4bre beneath it as he swam out. George clutched it des- pairingly,. and met with little resistance. He dived and rose with some young girl in his grasp, whom evenin that moment recognised as Lucy Raymond, the only daughter of the elderly pointsman, Jack Raymond, Collier'ii" mate." Hi hut was not far off, and George Collier had no time to lose. The rescue of the girl had not occupied .fire minutes from the time he had arrived upcsa the scene until the feat had been accom- plished, and he had scarcely time to regain the pointsraan's but. He hurried, therefore, to his own signal-ibox, shouting to the driver of the mineral train which was then approaching. The man did not appear to understand .the full meaning of the warning addressed to him, but ho comprehended that the signalman was improperly absent from his post, and; knowing that the I as- senger train was due, he slackened the speed of the train. Fortunate it was that he did so. The passenger train from the south had just arrived at the points, and Raymond,who was on duty, wouldrin another minute, have sent it,on to destruc- tion at full speed,' had not one of those unexplained interferences which men call " chance "- altered his intentions for the moment. A very common-place and, under worst circumstances, a most trivial inci- dent parented his workfrig for a few seconds He dropped his spectacles, and was obliged to stop and stoop and rub them before he felt he could open the shunting points. . The few seconds this little incident occupied-saved some Bees and many injuries. The passenger train came on indeed slowly • but the mineral 'train, warned.as the driver had been,was nearly at a stand- still when, the inevitable collision occurred at the points. Neither train left the metals, and no great damage-was :done to the rolling stock, but several passengers were shaken and one young man was incapable of all effort, He had been much cut about the headrand was carried to the pointsman's cottage close by to be attended to by the doctor. • Fortunately the accident was not a. serious one, and no claims were over made against the company, for in those happy days passengers had not studied the art of claiming damages, nor had the railway authorities learnt to ignore the convenience of indi- viduals.. Provision was made for those in any way injure& or inconvenienced; and consideratiourinpon one side met with appreciation on the other. George Collier helped to carry the wounded, man to Raymond's cottage, and the doctor found. two patients instead of one; but Lucy was quickly re- storedito her usual 'health, and attended upon the sick man, -whose eyes grew brighter,' and whose blood flowed more warmly when she approache& Had George Collier only guessed what misery his humane act would have caused him, he might have almost regretted. that the ,young tra.vellerladi been

It was 'accepted as such a thing of course that ,George and Lucy should go to church together, that no one hinted at any other ending to the lives of these , young people. George himself came over to the e cottage as an assured lover—a little too sure perhaps , —for girls do not like to be taken without 'trouble, nor do they care to be looked upen as be-ma to wed , where no formal or regular wooing has been carried ; om'for sweethearting is very dear to some feminine minds. THE MISER'S DIAMOND NECIMA.a.

tn the year 1740 there lived in the Latin quarter in a famons miser named Jean Avere. The wealth concealed in the obscure rookery where he resided was believed to be fabulous, and was no doubt really very great. Among his treasures was a celebrated dianaoud necklace of immense 'value. This he concealed so care- 'fully that he ultimately forgot its hiding-place himself. }3e sought diligently for weeks, and failing to Snd it, became almost insane. This rendered him ,even less capable of remembrance, and he took to his bed broken In' body as in mind. A few weeks later a doctor and an old woman, wholaad sometimes done odd jobs about his! house, were both at his bed-side seeing that the end ' Vaa near. As he clock in the neighbouring tower tolled one, he ceased his low muttering and 'sat up

and shrieked,'" I remember where it is now. I can pet

MY hand on the necklace. For God's sake let me go for it before I forget it again !" Here his weakness mad 'grcitement everca,me him, and he sankback among this Mds, stone dead. HORRIBLE OUTRAGES IN ARENA.

The " Dailyslterws "says—lntelligence from Van -states that Halil Paella, the governor, is constantly distributing Martini-Henry tines to the Kurdish chiefs of the district, with a view to assisting the Turkish troops to fall upon the Christain population in case of the rising which is sdreaded. The Kurds, emboldened by the encouragement they find in high places, are committing every kind of outrage and atrocity on the Armenian people. Feelings Of humanity have vanished, and the savage instincts of the Kurdish-shorde are causing ruin and desolation throughout the-ocuntry. On the 23rd May the Kurds outraged are Armenian woman of Kavish, and cut off her breasts with their swords. Death followed two hours, after the deed. The victim leaves three children. Again, insthe very towndf Van, a band of Turkish soldiers as-' saulted an inoffeasive woman who was on her way to church, with the result that she expired soon after. In tha beginning Of May, a Kurd, by name Kassim. killed a young Armenian in one of the principal thoroughfares of ;Van. Towards the middle of April inbre than fifteen Armenian villages at Haiotz-Tsor, south of V4n, were raided by the notorious Kurdish brigand chief Shakir and his numerous ibilowers. Halil Paella, the governor, has solar effected noarrests, and the alarm and terror for the lives and honour of families have assumed such pro- portions that an,ernigratlon en masse of Armenians is contemplated. Accounts of skirmishes between Armen- ians and Kurds 'in the vicinity of Van constantly peat in.

• ,ORANGE ?RIOTING IN ULSTER.

A riot took placeat Cookstown in connection with the First of :July celebrations on Tuesday night. A number of Oaange.lodges from the country came into town to accompany the locatiodges through the town, and, in returning to the old, town, met the lodge from Orriton, a suburb. 'The street through which they passed Is mainly inhabited by Catholics, and a crowd of women and girls assembled. About twenty police endeavoured to keep •the parties separate, but eventnally some of the Orange men and girls forced their way through the police, who were using their batons. The women in both crowds threw stones and shouted. One woman. a Protestant I named Rollins, complained to the police that her house 4iad been wrecked. Nearly every house in Onitor-street had windows broken, and Constable Henderson sus• tamed a severe cut on the lip. No other constables were injured, butimany civilians were struck by• stones or police batons. Order was not restored till nearly mid- night.

A ..JOIIRNRY at' A CASK.—At Leicester, on Monday, two glen, named George Mansfield and Arthur Hall, the latter described as a conjurer, were charged with cruelty to a donkey. Prisoners were travelling in a cask from Essex to Edinburgh, the cask beinglixed to a trolly drawn by a donkey. Hall lived in the cask. Councillor Muta,=in accordance with notice, asketh— '" Will the Chairman of the Paving and Sewerage Com- mittee assure this Council and the burgesses that his 'Committee will endeavour to obviate the nuisance ans.- ing frometenches in the public streets of the borough, •and the dangers tohealth caused by sewage gas founitin Trbrate dwellings 'and other places ? " Councillor Muir said there had been cause for complaint throughout the town. The streets were clean, but occasionally stenches 'offended the olfactory nerves of pedestrians. Two or three of the leadirig burgesses had sent letters to they 'newspapers complaining of the stench, and one gentle- man had actually threatened to leave the town if these defects were not remedied. He insinuated that the col- lective wisdom of the members of the Chamber was not very much to brag about. (Laughter.) He (Councillor; Muir) rather thought that that was an unwarrantable liberty to 'take with 'the Chamber. (Laughter., Other'', gentlemen complained of the presence of sewage gas in ''the-streets, and one gentleman had told him that the, wheleof his familyha,d on oneoccasion to leave the table ' and open the windows and doors owing to the entrance: Intothe house of-sewage gas. A member of the Council had said that at the top of Bewsey-st.—that street of .churches, chapels, and other respectable institutions-- the stench wasso over-powering that he would not like it repeated. Hethought thesewere matters which could • and should be •rectified. He was not • blaming the Borough Surveyor., nor did he say he was not doing his duty.. He shotild have additional assistance, as it was impossible for him to overlook the whole of the ontside work in.connection with his department. He had simply

brought the matter forward to ascertain the position of affairs. Within the last few hours he had heard that the Committee were most assiduously attending to these matters.

PAVING V. 113 SEWERAGE COIEHIMFB.-THE PAiING OF

BRIDGE-ST.-MEL MUIR'S QUESTION.

This Committee reported that the borough surveyor had submitted plans and specifications for the forming land paving of the following street and passage :-Back Crealey-st., and approach road to Back Crosley-st. The Committee recommended that the sewer in Forshaw-st. be eontinued to receive the drainage from the new houses recently erected by Mr. John Appleton. The minutes also contained;asopy of the memorial, signed by 52 owners and occupiers of property in Bridge-st., and 13 rate- payers using carriages, in favour of the paving of the upper part of that street with wood. The minutes were accompanied with a copy of the report of the borough surveyor on the paving ef Bridge-st., as given in full in our columns last week. The Committee after consider- ing the report decided that in view of the application ' already made to the County Council to declare the four main streets in the borough to be main roads within the ' meaning of the Local Government Act, 1888, the ques- tion of paving Bridge-st. be deferred for the present. , Alderman Davies, in moving the confirmation of the minutes of the Committee, first referred to the proposed 'wood paving in Bridge-st. He said a memorial had been presented on the subject. It was quite true that it had been determined—and confirmed by the Council— to pave the street with setts from "The Feathers" at Friars' Gate to Rylands-st., and it was no doubt the report of that fact appearing in thr newspapers which led to the memorial. The Borough Surveyor was in- structed to prepare a report upon the comparative cost of paving Bridge-st. with wood and setts, and he had done so. There was no material difference in the original cost. Although the members sympathized with the deputation in thinking that a wood pavement was more desirable than a sett pavement, it should be remembered that in a narrow street like Eridge-st. there were difficul- ties in 'the way of laying down wood. During the summer months a nuisance would arise owing to the stench from the wood and to the deposits upon it. It was also possible that other ratepayers would I feel aggrieved if a special pavement was put down for their convenience. Councillor Muir had done quite right in bringing forward the matter he had done. I He ,(Alderman Davies) wished to go into this question Ithoroughly, to satisfy the younger members of the 'Council, who were not aware of the trouble their seniors had experienced in this respect. In the year 1854 the the town was undermined by a number of rotten drains which were not worth the name of sewers, and the Corporation not only °Rained an Act of Parliament to ,overcome the existing difficulties, but called in aid of the ablest man of ,the times, to show them how the sewers should be made. He referred to Sir Robert Rawlinson —,(hear, hear)—who-aid there could be no absolute escape from the smells in or out of the houses owing to Warringten being such a low-lying town. Some of the sewers were in a fairly good condition, but there was not sufficient fall, and flushing operations had to be resorted to. And the Council knew the difficulties—the pecuniary difficulties—which existed with regard to that work. Acting upon the advice of Sir Robert Rawlinson, theyput down the best sewers they could in a low lying town. The time was coming when the Corporation wouldikave to sink a deep tank at the west end of the town—,say near Messrs. Monks and Co.'s works—and that would give them a sufficient fall. Bet the stench did not altogether arise from the sewers. (Hear, bean) , Sir Robert ''Rawlinson advocated the use of the grids whichallo wed the stench to escape into the Open air; and Pie danger there was not so great as it would be In the houses. (Hear, hear.) Alderman Davies pointed out. that the Committee were spending at the rate of £277 per annum in flushing sewers, with the object of avoiding the' stench. (Hear, hears) Alderman Wnrcumseconded the motion for the passing of therainutes. Councillor H. !WHEW'S said the facts the Alderman had given the Council would go a long way to allay a certain 'amount of anxiety which existed ameng the resi- dents of Museum-street and the adjoining streets with reference to the condition of the sewers. He did not think the blame was to be laid altogether upon the Pav- ing and Sewerage Committee. For three weeks, to his own personal knowledge, there had been a strong smell of gas at the end of Museum-street and St. Austin's-lane. Whether there was a leakage of gas or a deposit from the gas works he could not tell. With reference to the Bridge- street memorial, while there might be an objection to the paving of narrow streets withwood on sanitary grounds, that wags defect whichmight be got over by extra atten- tion, on the part of those whose duty it was to leek after the sanitary condition eel the borough. Bridge-street was aeliceelteepers' street, and the extraordinary traffic over it ought to be taken into consideration by the Committee with a view to providing as noiseless a pavement as possible. It would be better to retain the present macadam pavement rather than put doe-a setts. e(Elear, heay.) The time was coming when the four main -streets of the borough would have to be paved with admething different to what they were now paved with. 43ear, hear.) Councillor Mum said he was pleased and satisfied with tke explanation of AldermanOavies. Alderman EURGESS said that he also had been a:shop- keeper in 'Bridge-street for zonie years, a much longer time than Councillor Roberts, and he had sett paving all round, or as fur as it would go, his premises. There was noesointin Warrington where there was the same amount of braffic rolling over sett-pavement, and he was very pleased with it arid did not want a change. A woad pavement was a most unwholeseme pavement, and a macadamized pavement was a nuisance—it was either mud or dust. He was not in favemr of wood pavement

"(Laughter.) Alderman SUTTON Whether they walked back— (laughter)—or came back by boat or train. I think it would have been interesting to us all. (Hear, hear.) The lissoa (to Councillor Fleming) : Do you wish to reply? Councillor FLEMING : No. (Laughter.) The minutes were then adopted. THE DIUSEUIL—Mit. HOUGHTON'S GENEROUS GIFT.

In the minutes of the Museum, Library, and Arts Committee it was stated that during the month of May the visitors to the Museum were 2,681, an increase of 5 per cent, as compared with May, 1889 ; books issued in reading room 915, decrease 17 per cent. ; books issued in lending department 2,173, decrease 3 .per cent. The Committee also recorded. their thanks to Mr. W. D. Houghton for his gift of a picture by Clarence Whaite. Councillor MONKS in moving the adoption of the minutes, said that the Council would see in them a letter fgem Mr. W. D. Houghton offering to the Corporation a very beautiful picture as a gift from him to the town. (Hear, hear.) He was sorry that Mr. Robert Davies' letter acknowledging the gift had not been printed in the minutes, because he would like the Council to have seen how very appropriately it gave expression to the appreciation which the Corporation had of Mr. Hough- ton's aenerosity. (Applause.) The MAYOU said that the Town Clerk informed him that he knew nothing about the letter. It was not on the minutes of the meeting. Councillor MONKS said that at any rate the Council accepted the gift in the spirit in which it had been given. He thought Mr. Houghton had shown an amount of generosity and good will towards his fellow townsmen which was worthy of imitation. (Applause.) Councillor HENRY ROBERTS, in seconding the motion, endorsed every word Councillor Monks had said with regard to Mr. Houghton's generous gift. There was only one feeling, and that was of deep gratitude to Mr, Houghton. (Hear, hear.) The MAYOR said the Corporation all felt deeply grate- ful to Mr. Houghton for his generous gift. (Hear, hear.) Alderman Davins joinedin thanking Mr. Houghton for his noble offering. The minutes were adopted. The following is the letter, written by Mr. R. Davies, referred to above :—"Dear Sir,—l have now the pleasure of informing you that I have this afternoon laid your letter of the 9th instant before the Museum and Library Committee, and that the offer therein contained of Mr. Clarence Whaite's picture is most cordially accepted on the part of the Corporation. The Committee feel that the gift will be appreciated by every inhabitant of the town, and that the work will form a welcome and most valu- able addition to the Art Gallery. lam instructed to add that Mr. Chorley's assistance in finding a suitable Posi- tion for the picture will be gladly received. lam also instructed to inform you that a vote of thanks was passed by acclamation at our meeting.—l am, dear sir, yours very faithfully, ROBERT Davms, Chairman of the Museum and Library Committee. W. D. Houghton, Esq., The Beeches, Moore, Warrington, 13th July, 1890."

THE CEMETERY.

From the report of the registrar to the Cemetery Com- mittee it appeared that the interments for the past five weeks were :—Adults, 43; minors, 57; stillborn and premature, 10. And for the corresponding period last year :—Adults, 41 ; minors, 52; stillborn and permature, 12.—The receipts for the past five weeks we e £99 145., and for the corresponding period last year £7B 18s. ed. BATILS.-THE PkIIIE. SWINGS.

The Town Hall, Park, and Baths Committee stated that from the report of the bath-keeper it appeared that the income from the baths during the past four weeks was £3B 15s. 10d., and for the corresponding period last year £4B 12s. 6d. At a meeting of the Committee, a deputa- tion from the Infirmary Committee attended and explained their views in full with respect to the land required for the proposed extension. After considering the same and visiting the site, it was resoled" That the Committee are prepared to recommend the Council t ) grant to the Trustees of the Warrington Infirmany a plot of land adjoining the Infirmary, containing about 438 square yards, according to the amended plan, now , Approved by the Committee." It was stated that the grant was subject to the approval of the Treasury.—At asubsequent meeting, Mr. W. Owen, architect, attended and submitted the plans for the extension of the Infir- mary, which were examined and approved. The MAYOR moved the confirmation of the minutes of the Committee. Councillor BOLTON seconded.. Councillor JENKINS enquired if anythigg was being done with a view to improving thebath accommodation. He said he had heard complaints with regard to the con- dition of the baths. Councillor Dr. CANNELL remarked that the Council were making a very great mistake in allowing one inch of the park to be taken from them. They had a park of 121 acres, which was well built round, and be take a yard of that land was like drawing a tooth. (Hear, hear). The Infirmary Committee had simply crammed down their throats the fact that the Infirmary was a charitable institution, and that giving land to the Infirmary was assisting workingmen. He denied it. It was a piece of downright robbery to the ratepayers at large. If the Infirmary Committee wanted to make an extension they could buy property for a very trifling sum, by which they could make extensions for the next 40 or 50 years. They were now only making room for some 16 or 20 patients, and the present extension was simply a prim- ary one for six or eight years. Then they would come to the Corporation again, and would be bound to have further land. He entered his protest against the con- cession, as he coati.dered it a piece of injustice to the ratepayers. Alderman HARRISON said he did not regret the conces- sion, so long as the land was being applied to so excel- lent a purpose. Alderman BURGESS agreed wi;li Alderman Harrison that the Infirmary was doing a grai.'d work. Tkat could not be doubted for a moment by aiy pOrSon of bane mind. At the same time,he sympathised,to soilne Want, with Councillor Cannel!. He could not hep thl‘aktog that a mistake had been made, both on the part of Council and the Infirmary Committee' in selecting the site on which the Infirmary was built, and he thought that had they had the experience at that time they had at the present, another site would have been chosen. Under the present circumstances, however, if the Infirm- ary Committee wanted more land they were bound to assist them. It was to him a grave question whether the money that had been generously and freely subscribed for the extension ought to be laid out in extending the institution further on the site it now occupied. He did not think it wise for the Council to surrender any portion of that land which had been set aside as a public park for the inhabitants, either for the Infirmary or anything else. They had just spent a good sum in putting down a noiseless pavement in front of the institution, but trains were running every quarter of an hour along the railway a little further off, and they vibrated and shook the whole ef the building. And then, again, on the other side, there was the rattle of the musketry in the Drill Hall.

Councillor HENRY ROBERTS asked what was the idea in paving the gymnasium in the park with flags? He did not know whether the Committee were aware that a boy had suffered serious injury in consequence of the flags being there. Was it the idea of the Committeethat they should take out the value of the land they were giving by providing patients for the Infirmary? (Laughter.) Councillor Bourox said that as to the flags at the gymnasium the Committee knew nothing about that. With reference to the baths, if any person had com- plaints to make he should be glad if they would make them at theCommitteemeetings, when the matters would be thoroughly sifted. Councillor JENKINS said that the Council Chamber was the only place where he was privileged to make the matter known, and he should certainly not be dictated to by Councillor Bolton or the Chairman or Vice-Chairman of any Committee. He would, however, now take care that personal complaints should be brought before the Committee. Alderman HOLMES said he agreed with Councillor Cannell on the subject of the transfer of the land. At the time the Infirmary was built, he objected to any part of the park being alienated from its original object, and he hoped that now the land was to be given it would be the very last piece. Alderman DAvizs said that the land which was being given the Infirmary, was really only what one would call a dirty back garden. If they took the value of that piece of land at the price they gave for it, even then the Cor- poration would not be subscribing to the Infirmary at the rate which some other employers were. The MAYOR said that if any improvement could be made in the condition of the baths the Committee would see that it was done. So far as the gymnasium was con- cerned, Mr. Longdin explained that there were no swings in the place, and the only accident had been a fall from a vaulting horse. The minutes were adopted. GENERAL PURPOSES COMMITTEE.—THE LATCH.

FORD BRIDGES AND THE WATERWORKS PIM-

CHASE. The minutes of the Committee contained a copy of the petition against the London and North-Western Railway Company's Bill, which it was resolved be approved, sealed, and presented to Parliament. At another meet- ing of the Committee, it was resolved that Mr. J. L. Tunstall, F.C.A., be requested to examine and report upon the accounts of the Warrington Waterworks Com- pany, for the last three years. Mr. F. W. Monks at- tended the Committee, and objected on behalf of Monks, Hall, and Company, Limited, to the inclusion of their works in the proposed extension of the borough boun- dary. After hearing the representations made by Mr. Monks, the Committee intimated their willingness to place the property of the company on a differential rat- ing for fire years after the'passing of the Bill. The Com- mittee also decided to lodge a petition against the Man- chester Ship Canal (Various Powers) Bill. At a meeting of the Committee, held on the 23rd June, it was resolved that the report of Mr. Mansergh, C.E., on the plant and works of the Warrington Waterworks Company be ap- proved and printed. The Maxoa moved the confirmation of the minutes of the Committee, and said I have pleasure in reporting that in accordance with the instructions of the General Purposes Committee, the witnesses for the Corporation

attended to give evidence before the Committee, of the House of Lords on.the Manchester Ship Canal (Various Powers) Bill. An interview was arranged between the representatives of tile Corporation and those of the Cana Company, at which a satisfactory arrangement was arrived at, and which was afterwards agreed to in the Commiteee of the House. The Bill as altered provides: 1. That the swing:bridges over Knutsford-read and the Northwich-road shall be at not less than 36 feet be- tween the parapets. 2. The provisions of sub-section 1 of section 88 of the Act of 1885, which provide for the opening and closing of the bridges subject to certain pen- alties, are extended to these bridges. 3. Sub-sections 8, 10, 20, and 22 of the same section are made part of the Bill. 4. The new railway bridge over Wash-lane is to be removed and replaced by a bridge 36 feet wide ,between the abutments. 5. Theapproach road leading from Wish-lane to Acker's-lane on the south of the Canal is to be 36 fevt wide, with a footpath six feet wide on either side. 6. The approach road from Grappenhall- road and Hunt's-lane, on the south side of the Canal, is to be 30 feet wide, including a footway on one side thereof six feet wide. The bridge will be 25 feet wide. The roads and bridges are to be made and completed to the reasonable satisfaction of the Borough Surveyor. The swing-bridges at Grappenhall-road and Acker's-lane have been abandoned, I think the Corporation is to be congratulated upon the satisfactory arrangement made with the company, and I believethe concessions made by the company will be found of great public bene- fit.

—Alderman HARRISON said the Council had reason to congratulate themselves upon the final adjustment of the difficulties which a short time ago presented themselves. Councillor FLOOD, referring to the report of Mr. Man- sergh, on the plant and works of the Warrington Water Works Company, contended that whatever investigation took place in c mriection with the Water Works should be of the most searching character. (Hear, hear.) Yet Mr. Mansergh told them that the examination was merely a superficial one. One paragraph of the report said: —"lt was explaieed to me, by Mr. Nicholson, in a statement assented to by Mr. Longdin, that the examina- tion of the company's works which I was about to make was to be merely a superficial examination: that they were not to be called upon to open out any buried works, nor to make any trial pumping; that a copy of my report was to be furnished to them, and that the whole proceeding was to be without prejudice." The report told them a little furthe,r on that the pipes were said by Mr. Ross to be in a good condition, and in the same breath Mr. Mansergh said: "I saw several samples in Mr Longdin's office of pipes taken out, of the system which were considerably diminished in sectional area, both by corrosion and incrustation." TEE MAYOR said that had been withdrawn. Those pipes had not been taken out of the waterworks system It was believed they had been taken out of some portion' of Cockhedgc Mills, and brought by Mr. Diggle and put into Mr. Longdin's hands. (Laughter.) Councillor nook thought the report sliould have been corrected before it got into the hands of the Council. He contended that they were showing less discretion in handing over a quarter of a million of the town's money than they would exercise if they were handing over their own. He honestly believed that if they allowed the re- port to pass without a further investigation they would not be carrying out the principle which was intended iza buying the concern.

' Councillor BOLTON thought the report of Mr. Ma.nsergh was rather one-sided, as the examination had been super- ficial only, and not a thorough one. The minutes were then adopted, and the meeting ter- minated.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting of this Board was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday morning. Alder- man Harrison presided, and there were also present Rev. E. C E. Carleton; Messrs: T. Jones, Heaton, Davies, Gill, Eadowes, Smith, W. Boughton, Mather, Turton, Humphreys. Ratcliffe, .T. Houghton, Auk- land, Kilsball, Balharry, flood-Wright, A. Jolley; J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), C. H. Ward (Workhouse master), and H. M. Mines (super- intendent, Padgate Schools).

THE INFIRMARY AND PAUPER PATIRNTS. The CHAIRMAN referred to the question of the admit- tance of pauper patients to the Warrington Infirmary in cases of emergency, and said he had seen the hon. treasurer of that institutiOn who replied, as he (the Chairman) had anticipated, that no matter whether the patients were paupers or npt they should certainly deal with them. He, therefore, apprehended that that would meet the views of the Guardians and that there would be no furtber difficulties. (Hear, hear.)—Mr. T. JONES : It is very gatisfactory, Mr. Chairman. ELECTION EXPENSES. PASSAGE AT ARMS BETWEEN THE CHAIRMAN AND THE CLERK.

On the minutes of the previous meeting being read, the CHAIRMAN referred to a sum of £5 mentioned therein as having been paid to the Clerk in connection with the recent elections. He thought that at the previous meet- ing when the subject was mentioned that there had been three vacancies, but it turned out that there were only two vacancies? Was he right in saying that there were only two vacancies ?—Mr. RATCLIFFE :That was so.—The CLERK said he hoped no gentleman was led to believe anything to the contrary. He was sorry that it always happened there was never a word on that subject except when the Chairman was present. He hoped they would refer the whole thing to a committee.—The Cnanizem did not wish to intrude any observation calculated to offend anyone. He was simply making an inquiry to which there ought to be no difficulty in hav- ing a reply. He wanted to know what the £5 was \ for.—The CLERK pointed out that the work of the returning officer was dealt with in the consolidated orders, and the auditor would not allow any pay- ment to be made that was not strictly legal. Evolution. Tim redemption of the masses.in in relation to present life is Co-operation.. blem which occupies, quite as much attentiomnow-a-days a3,redemptiork in rela- tion to-thelife,toi come. Unfortunately, there, is in bothecases ne small theexising, aucli no small uncertainty as. to how that redemptiisn can beat be accomplished. It was believed by, a large sectionsof people that the salvation of humanity, so far as this world goes, must be sought in,CO- - BLit Co-operation has not,achieved all that WAS expected of it by its sanguine advocates; 1 and there are some reformers who,have reached the conclusiOn that while the principle a Co- operation, is all right, ta be effertive the State must, step in and make it compulsory. This is the doctrine of the extreme section of the Socia- lista,-; but,, as their proposals involve,. to begin wit,eonfiseation of exiSting capital, they do not find, much favour in this country; with thought- fut, fair-minded men.. What is, called Com- pulsory Co-operation, is promulgated in the Vl:kited States; and, as will be seen. from an able article in another column,Mr. ILL WILMEAM, of San Etaneisco, and formerly of Warrington, has been Ic.embating the propesal, and at thesame tune put- ting in a word in behalf of the further trial of Toluntary Co-operation. Mr. WI:11111AM takes the. ,editor of the " Patific Union " to task for saying 'that" Co-operation as a means of relieving the ; miseries of the working-men of Christendom is thus far a dead Totter." As, an Englishman famili- ar with what has been done in this country,, and particularly in Warrington, Mr. Wilirrum is able to make out a good ease in favour otCo- operation as applied to the distribution of pro- ducts ; but be admits that comparatively little has been done in the directien of Co-operatvie produc- tion. He holds, however, that Distribution and Protection are little by little approaching each other ; and he still looks to "Co-operation, aided by the voluntary effort of all parties," as "the panacea for all our ills." This is a large faith, and the foundation of it is that "the employer is, and must be, as much dissatisfied with the present state of things as the employe." But is this so I' We must confess to feeling somewhat sceptical as to the feeling of the em- ployer upon the subject. The manufacturers of the world are naturally dissatisfied when their pro- fits are reduced, or when losses are occasioned by strikes ; but the dissatisfaction has not, except in a very few cases, led them to say :—" We are tired of this warfare; and henceforward you as work- people shall share the profits with us." They may

===== 0002601_18900705_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
I

new and then think of such a thing ; but they' reetet, too,that as losses cannot very well be shared as well, as profits, the new game is not worth the candle. We have not much hope, therefore, of progress on this side of the: question, unless Labour should becemeso genevally and so highly organised as to be abrato compel Capital in its own intelests to take Labe& into partnership. THUNDERSTORY AT RUNCORN.

A thunderstarm of considerable violence, but of short duration occurred at Bunccrn, on Saturday morning. Fortunately no damage Was done. VISIT OF LIVERPOOL ONANGENEN TO. HALTON.

The members of the Orange Lodges of Liverpool intend to pay a visit to Halton Castle on the 14th inst., and Mr. Woolstoncroft, the landlord of the Castle Hotel, has ob- tained a licehse to sell intoxicating liquors in the grounds attached to his house. tINIVERSITT EXTENSION LUCTITTLES AT IttINCORN.

The annual meeting 'of those interested in the Univer- sity Extension scheme in Runcorn, will be held in the Commissioners Board room, at the Town Hall, on Mon- day evening. Mr. S. Taylor will preside. Full particu- lars will be found in our advertising columns.

• SUDDEN DEATH AT RUNCORN. • An inquest was held at the Burial Board Office, Run- corn, on Tuesday, on the body of Godfrey Nutter, a boatman, who was found dead in his boat on Sunday. The evidence showed that the man had been ill for some time, and a verdict of" Natural causes "'was returned. Mr. Worsley, the coroner, was present. UMBRELLAS RE-COVERED on the premtses at J. & W. Dutton's, Sankey-st.—Adrt. WIDNES PARISH CHURCH. _ _

The annual sermons in aid ofthe National Schools were held on Sunday, at St. Mary's Parish Church and St. Paul's Church. At the former place the Rev. J. Wright Williams, vicar of Rainford, preached in the morning; and the Rev. Dr. Preston, M.A., vicar of Holy Trinity, Runcorn, in the evening. At St. Pauls' the preachers were the Rev. G. H. Garrett, vicar, and the Rev. J. Wright Williams. The various services were well attended, and a crowded congregation assembled to hear Mr. Williams in the evening. The offertories realised a satisfactory sum.

_ FLOWER SERVICE AT RUNCORN.

The annual flower service in connection with Bethesda Congregational Chapel was held on Sunday last. The sacred edifice was appropriately decorated for the occa- sion with plants and flowers. In the morning the Rev. John Simpson, the pastor, preached a sermon entieed "The Garden of the Soul," and in the afternoon the choir and scholars rendered the serviceof song" Primrose Garth," the narrative being read by Mr. Simpson. 'The Rev. Mr. Cole, of Garston, occupied the pulpit in the evening, when a good congregation assembled. Collec- tions were taken in aid of the funds.

WIDNES SCHOOL BOARD.

The monthly meeting was held at the Town Hall, on Tuesday afternoon. Mr. T. Sutton Timmis pre- sided, and there were present the Rev. Father Clark, Dr. O'Keeffe, Messrs. H. Wade Deacon, B. Brown, S. Quinn, and G. H. Danby (clerk). The District Education Committee reported that during June there were 143 applications for school fees, which were recommended to be paid in 126 cases. 120 persons appeared before the Committee fcr contraven- tion of the Education Act,and 26 were prosecuted before the magistrates, 21 convicted, and 5 boys sent to the Truant School. The Finance Committee reported that the receipts for June amounted to £1,319 95., including £1,200 received from the overseers on account of pre- cept. Accounts amounting to £765 Is. Bd. were recom- mended for payment. The minutes were approved.

A MERSEY MYSTERY AT WIDNES.

_ On Wednesday morning, at the Mersey Hotel, Widnes, Mr. W. T. Husband, the deputy coroner, held an inquest on the body of a man who was found in the Mersey at Hale Head, on Sunday.—John Lewis, of Hunter-st., Liverpool, identified the body as that of Mr. Lee, who had lodged at his house from the 27th April to the 16th May. He was a farmer from America, and arrived in England on the former date. The deceased was in low circumstances and had to leave the house of witness because he had no money. He said he expected some money from America, but none came. The deceased seemed very depressed in spirits and had used such expressions as "might as well be dead" and "might as well be drowned." . Witness believed the deceased was last seen alive on Sunday week on the Landing Stage at Liverpool bya Liverpool tobacconist named McDonnell. —Thomas Lomax, boatman, Widnes, deposed to findity, the body in the river„ and a verdict of "Found drowned" was returned. " SCUTTLERS " AT RUNCORN.

Before Mr. James Handley and Mr. G. L. Wigg, at Runcorn Petty Sessions on Monday, Fred Johnson, a young man, was charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Duke-st., and also with assaulting P.C. Hulse, whilst in the execution of his duty. About a quarter past ten on Saturday night P.C. Hulse found the prisoner at the entrance to the Theatre Royal. He was without coat and hat, and was struggling with the door keeper. Hulse seized the defendant, who gave him a violent blow in the face, and then the defendant's friends knocked him to the ground and kicked and thumped him. The defendant bit the officer on the leg, causing *serious wound.—Mr. Superintendent Large said the •defendant was one of a gang of young men who went about molest- ing people, and in Salford they were called " Scuttlers." The defendant was fined ss. and costs for being drunk, and 10s. and costs for the assault, or 21 days' hard labour.

===== 0002601_18900705_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
A FRODSIIAM FELONY CASE.

At Cheshire Quarter Sessions, on Monday, Ellen Cooper, a respectable young woman, who had been admitted out on bail, surrendered upon an indictment charging her with having stolen a watch from a servant named Annie Atherton, at Frodsham, on the 11th April. Mr. Yates prosecuted. The prisoner was a friend of the prosecutrix, and on the evening of the day in question prosecutrix hung up her watch on a nail in the kitchen. Prisoner called upon her, and after a short stay went away. Prosecutnx missed the watch, and did not see the prisoner for a couple of days after, and when she accused her of stealing the watch she said if she could not get a watch without stealing she would not wear one.—Prisoner now admitted her guilt.—Mr. Austin, prisoner's late master, said she had been in his service for the last four years, during wnich time she had conducted herself properly. He was willing to take her back again. —Prisoner was discharged, but was bound over in a surety of £lO to come up for judgment when called upon. UMBRELLAS RE-COVERED on the premises at J. & W. Dutton's, sankev-st.—Advt.

THE_ATTACS ON SHIP CANAL MEN AT FRODSHAM.

On Monday, at Cheshire Quarter -gessions, Thomas Birchwood (34,) labourer, Frodsham, was charged with being concerned with seven other men in ill- treating some of the men employed on the Ship Canal on the 19th March. Mr. E. H. Lloyd prosecuted, and said the facts of the case were well-known to the Bench, as four men who were engaged in the assault were dealt with at the last Sessions. They were sentenced to three months' imprisonment. Prisoner absconded, and as not arrested until a few days ago. Witnesses were callbd who proved that prisoner was one of the gang. Prisoner admitted being with the gang, and was sentenced to three months' imprisonment with hard labour.—Edward Booth (23), labourer, Frodsham, was charged with a similar offence'on the same day, and the prisoner, on the advice of his counsel, Mr. Malcolm Douglas, pleaded guilty to having" coinmitted a common assault. The Rev. • Edward Percy Alexander, Manley', gave the prisoner a good:character, and urged the Court not to make a gaol bird of him. The Court, taking into consideration some of the facts of the case, sentenced the prisoner to one month's hard labour. '
