THE EXAMINEIt-SATURDAY,,JULYf 5, 1890

POLICE CHANGES AT RIINCOEIL

_ After being at Preston Brook for four years, P.C. Jack- son has been removed to Alderley Edge, where he will be Acting-Sergeant. P.C. Brew has been transferred from Runcorn to Preston Brook. . RUNCORN POLICE COURT.

At this Court on Thursday, William Powell was sen- tenced to seven days' hard Moth for begging. John ' Smith was charged with having stolen a pair of boots worth 25., the property of Abraham Clucas. The prisoner was discharged for want of evidence. Yester- day Alexander Malcolmson, of Park Gate, who presented a distressed appearance, was charged withhaving,on the previous day,attempted to defraud the London and North- Western Railway Company. At noon he was found in a North Wales train at Runcorn, having in his possession a ticket from Liverpool to Edge Hill. As the prisoner had been convicted for a similar offence, and was "wanted" on other charges he was remanded. s

£1,079 19s. or in round numbers £3,000 less. The Author- ity had not had any notii;efrom Warrington Corporation of their proposal in any shape or form, nor was their any mention in the Bill of the Authority or its sewers, nor were the sewers shown upon the plan. He had written to his parliamentary agents in London asking in what position the Bill was in Parliament, and he had received the following reply :—" We are in receipt of your letter. This BiU stands referred to the Police and sanitary Committee of the House of Commons, and is the last in the group, and we cannot say when it will be reached. It is too late to petition in the first House, and we cannot yet say when petitions will be due in the Lords." The Clerk suggested that he should lay the simple facts of the case before his Parliamentary agents and ask them *hat course they should pursue. As the Corporation had not given them any notice, he did not think they were out of the Contnons.—The Chairman: We don't intend to sit down quietly andlet them take a por- tion of the township.—Mr.Aaron: Certainly not.—Mr. Mc- Intyre moved that the Clerk beinstructed to communicate to his Parliamentary agents the circumstances of the case, and ask their advice.—Mr. Aaron seconded the proposi- tion, which was carried unanimously. THE POLLETION OF KEKENTICK BROOK. The Clerk reported that in accordance with instructions received, he had forwarded a copy of the resolution passed at the last meeting, and a copy of the thialysis of the water of Kekewick Brook to Mr. W. R. Earp,of Pres- ton Brook tannery, and he had received no reply.—Mr. Aaron contended that according to the resolution he pro- posed at the last meeting, the Clerk should have taken proceedings before this for the abatementof the nuisance. He asked was it right that the farmers should pay rent, rates, and taxes, for land, and not be able to turn their cattle upon it. He was certain that the brook was in a worse staCe now—and he had not seen it nor heard of it— than it was when the samples were taken. The old pits were filled up with refuse from the tannery, and this wet weather the sulphuretted hydrogen was sent into the brook, which would be worse to-morrow than it was to-day. The Clerk ought to havetaken more severe steps. —Mr. Whitlow said he could quite bear out what Mr. Aaron had said. He never saw the brook in a worse state than it had been during the past week.—Mr. Aaron: As long as,this wet weather continues,the water will wash the poison out of the pits and the brook will become worse every day. He considered that the Clerk ought to have taken more strong steps to have the nuisance remedied. —The Assistant Clerk said the Authority would have to obtain permission from the Local Government Board be- fore they could take proceedings.—Mr. Aaron moved that they apply to the Local Government Board for the neces- sary authority.—Mr. Hewitt seconded the proposition, which was carried unanimously.—Mr. W. Whitlow moved that the Inspector take two more samples of the water in the brook.—Mr. Aaron seconded, and remarked that they should be taken that afternoon.—Carried. This was the whole of the business.

RUNCORN AND THE CHESHIRE POLICE INQUIRY. A public meeting of the inhabitants of Runcorn, summoned by hand bills, was held in Bethesda Congregational School, on Friday night, for the pur- pose of taking into consideration the serious allega- tions made against the township ifs the case of O'Donnell v. the Chief Constable of Cheshire, at the inquiry now proceeding at Chester. There was a good audience, and Mr. J. J. Howett was voted to the chair. Amongst those present were Mews. W. Shaw (seamen's missionary), J. Jepson, T. Jones, A. Hough, H. Coward, S. Yarwood, P. Smith, W. Han- kinson, Jonathan Horstield, R. Abel, J. Abel, J. Davies, Dr. McDougall, R. Boyle, G. Torrie, E. Field, J. Rayner, Walton, W. Marsh, J. Knowles, Guest, J. W. Lightburn, J. Duttqn, L. Holmes, Watson, W. H. Chadwick, Ileatherington, W. Betney, l. unison, I. Stubbs. The Chairman said he had been in Runcorn nearly three years, and he had watched the habits of the people perhaps more closely than an older inhabitant; he had gone through the streets at almost all hours of the night; and he thought Runcorn compared very favourably indeed with other populous places. (Applause.) In fact, compared with some of the large cities that he had seen and lived in, Runcorn was a great deal better—at least to outward appearance—so far as regarded the drinking customs. He admired a public officer who did his duty, and he would be the first to clap a man on the back and say "go on," but there was a difference between a man doing his duty and poking his nose into other folks business, whereby he had continually got himself into hot water. This was what Inspector O'Donnell did with the people of Runcorn. There was no working-man,' either teetaller or moderate drinker, or drunkard, who liked the appearance of that man. They knew what sort of a name he went by—whether it was righteously deserved or not—and if they Imd asked for O'Donnell probably no one would have known him. He thought O'Donnell would have acted vrisaly if he had defended himself, and not have given utterance to so many of his bad impressions of the people of Runcorn. He had said , they were drunken. He (the Chairman) had refrained from taking strong drink for as many years as O'Donnell was old, and he could tell when a man was drunk as well as O'Donnell, and he could can- didly say that generally the people of Runcorn were sober. Unfortunately there were drunkards in Run- corn, same as there were anywhere else, but It would be an everlasting disgrace to the ministers of religion if Runcsrn was such a disreputable small town as described, after they had spent so many years in trying to reclaim the wicked. O'Donnell had also said the town was in a 'very immoral state. and that there were a great many brothels. Ile did not know of his per- sonal knowledge that there was one ; but from careful inquiry that there were very few of that class of women in Runcorn. If O'Dohnell knew there were brothels in Runcorn it was his pkrticular business to deal with them 11 and if he had not done that. he had failed in his duty. (Applause). He moved "That this meeting emphatic- ally protests against the aspersions cast upon the inhab- itants of the township of Runcorn at the inquiry now proceeding in the ease of O'Donnell venue the Chief Constable of Cheshire, and they deeply regret that the following statements by O'Donnell were received by the 1 same Committee. The town was in a very immoral state and there were a great many brothels. Also this meeting solemnly protests against the allegations of excessive drunkenness in the town and most strongly denies that this statement is true, and this meeting deeply regrets that this statement was received by the Committee unchallenged." (Applause).—Dr. McDougall seconded the resolution, which he said he did not think was sufficiently strong. He felt sure that the working classes of Runcorn would resent in the strongest man- ner the allegations made With reference to them. He had a personal acquaintance of 22 years with Runcorn, and he never observed that the working classes or people of any description were otherwise than well . behaved, and would compare very favourably indeed in other communities. (Applause.) There were 3,000 or more workingmen connected with the secret orders of the town, making provision for a rainy day, for themselves and their families ; and out of the 660 owners' of pro- perty in the township, 300 were artisans ljvjn hi their own /loves. (Applause.) In the Trustee Saving.l4trik and thd 'Post Office Savings Bank there was no Pas a sum than £48,000 invested to the credit of the working classes. (Applause.) While in one building society in the town over £20,000 was invested by the industrial classes. flow inconsistent were these facts with the state- ments of O'Donnell. In regard to the alleged drunken- ness and profligacy, Dr. McDougall quoted the following statistics. In 1885, 11 publicans and beersellers were prosecuted and seven convicted; two prostitutes were prosecuted and convicted. In 1886, 7 publicans pro- secuted, 5 convicted, 4 prostitutes convicted, and one brothel keeper convicted. In 1887 there were 11 publi- cans prosecuted and 7 convicted ; 4 brothel keepers were prosecuted and convicted ; and 5 prostitutes were pro- secuted and 4 convicted. In 1888, On May of which year Inspector O'Donnell entered into Runcorn-) there were 13 publicans prosecuted and 10 convicted; 3 brothel keepers were prosecuted and convicted ; and 16 prosti- tutes were prosecuted and 15 convicted. In 1889 there were 21 publicans prosecuted and 7 convicted— (laughter and applause)-4 brothel keepers were prosecuted and 3 convicted ; while 21 prostitutes were prosecuted and 16 convicted. Dr. Mc- Dougall contended that the figures refuted in the most unmistakable manner, the allegations that had been levelled against the township. (Applause.) As to the drunkenness there had always been a large floating population in connection with the docks ; and since 1888 there had been a large migratory population introduced into the town who had swelled the ranks of those prosecuted for drunkenness. At the present time there were about 1,000 added to the resident population of the township. In 1886, there were 156 persons pro- secuted for drunkenness; lBB7, 175; 1888, 344 ; 1889, 528. In comparing those figures they must have regard to the character of the people they had to deal with in addition to the population, and he contended that the statement that the people a Runcorn were drunken had not been made out. The people as a rule were more sober than they could find in any own of its size in the kingdom. (Applause.) As to brothels, he took credit to himself of having drawn the attention of the Local Authority to the powers conferred upon individuals by the Criminal Law Amendment Act, therefore the state- ment by Inspector O'Donnell that this question was neglected until he came was absolutely untrue. He drew attention to the large number of sailors and boat people who came to the port, and said surely they would not visit en the heads of the resi- dent population all the offences of the floating popula- tion. (Hear, hear.) He did not think, however, that the putlic would attach much importance to state- ments that were not being made on oath, and he commented strongly upon the large amount of money I an inquiry of this description was costing the ratepayers. —Mr. James Cashm, an ex-police Constable, said that meeting was entirely out of place. (Hear, hear.) They were attempting to take the case out of the hands of the County Council—(No)—and he appealed to those present to give Inspector O'Donnell fair play. He had served in the force and knew the tyranny that had been used, and he considered that this was the best inquiry ever held by any public body. (Applause.) He moved that the meet- ing be postponed until the trial was decided. (Hear, hear.)—Mr. William Betnev seconded.—Mr. Jepson pro- tested against the attempt by O'Donnell to degrade their

town, and he said it was nothing but over officiousness 'that brought him into the position he now occupied. (Applause.) Six only voted for the amendment, and the Chairman declared the resolution carried by an over- whelming majority. (A Voice; Of publicans and sinners, laughter.) Mr. .Jepson then moved "That this meeting is of opinion that the money spent on this inquiry by the Cheshire County Council is absolutely a waste of public money, and wholly unjust, and that a copy of both reso- lutions be forwarded to Sir Horatio Lloyd,chairmati of the Committee of Inquiry."—Mr. S. Yarwood seconded.— Mr. Cashin proposed an amendment that a vote of thanks be given to the County Council for the honest and straightforward way in which they had done their duty. —Mr. Fletcher seconded the amendment, which was de- feated by a large majority.—A vote of thanks to the Chairman concluded the proceedings.

RUNCORN BOARD OF COMMISSIONERS. The monthly meting was held at the Town Hall on Thursday. Mr. S. Taylor presided, and there were present Messrs. Meadowcroft, Fisher, Wiswall, Hankinson, Pritchard, Lightburn, White, Mellor, Morris, Wass, F. A. Lake (law clerk), E. Marshall (secretary), and M. Barker (surveyor). HEALTH OF THE TOWN. The Medical Officer of Health (Dr. MacDougall), re- ported that the ntnnber of deaths registered during June amounted to 28. • Of these one was an inquest case, and of the remaining deaths, 6 were due to convulsions, 3 each to phthisis, bronchitis, heart disease, premature birth, natural decay, 2 to mesenteric disease, 2 to inflam- mation of the lungs and one each to kidney disease and diarrhoea. The 28 deaths showed an average death-rate of 18.6 per thousand per annum. There had been Several cases of scarlet fever during the past month, but they had been of a mild type.—The Surveyor remarked that in the corresponding month last year there were 35 deaths.

WHO IS AFRAID? Mr. Pritchard moved that it be an instruction to the secretary to furnish a return of the attendances of mem- bers at the various Board meetings and Committee meetings during the year, he laid before the Board at the meeting held in August of each year. He thought It would be an advantage to the ratepayers to learn how their representative had attended to the duties they had undertaken to perform.—Mr. Lightbum pointed out 1 that some members were on more committees than others, and he considered that the only fair way was to give the percentage of attendance.—Mr. Mellor sug- gested that the matter should be left over for 12 months, as he thought it would be unfair to take the members on the hop in this way. He was not afraid that his average attendance would be detrimental to his char- acter in any way.—The Chairman: I come out this year but lam not afraid. (laughter.) lam sorry for those members who are afraid to answer to the public 1 for the duties which they agreed to perform.—Mr. Meedoweroft pointed out that according to the Stand - I lug Orders the motion could not be dealt with, becatite the necessary notice had not been given; but it was LI understood that the Secretary would prepare the list. and submit it at the neNt meeting.

A HORSE AMBULANCE FOR WIDNES. The necessity of having a horse ambulance in which to convey the maimed to the Accident ?los- pita!, and the sick to the Disease Hospital, in Widnes has been felt for a considerable period, and a few weeks ago a number of tradesmen and mem- bers of the public force formed themselves into a committee to devise some means of attaining the desired end. Eventually a cricket match between the tradesmen and police was arranged, and the price of admission being fixed at a low figure, the tickets were disposed of with gratifying rapidity, and when the accounts are made up, it is expected that upwards of £BO of the £lOO required will have been obtained. The match was fixed for Thursday and the weather fortun- ately being fine, there was a large assemblage of specta- tors, who witnessed an interesting game. Although defeated, the police made a good fight ag,ainst their more practiced opponents, and it is quite on the cards that Wolfenden would have had he honour of being the high- est scorer at the match, had he not been struck on the bead by a ball, wbi.;ll placed t„'crii 4s egliaMt for. some time, and he succumbed to the first ball he receiveu on his recovery. Mr. C. Neil, pawnbroker, offered a rite of a gold pendant to the highest scorer in the match, which was secured by Carroll, of the tradesmen. For the best scores in the policemen's eleven there were offered a gold signet ring, given:by Mrs. Jameson, pawn- broker ; a set of gold solitaires, given by Mr. Pownall, watchmaker ; and a pair of fancy slippers (to measure) given by Mr. Hicks. bootmaker. RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD,

The monthly meeting was'beld in the Town Hall, on Tuesday evening. Mr. J. Pritchard presided, and there were present the Itev. Dr. Gastaldi, Dr. McDougall. Mr. A. Salkeld, M. S. Taylor, and Mr. 11. Moss (clerk). It was reported that the certified Government accom- modation, was 3,287; total number on the rolls, 3,397; total average attendance, 28866; percentage of atten- dances, 85. During the month 50 parents have appeared before the School Attendance Committee, and the officers , have served 492 warning notices for irregular atten- dance.—Dr. McDougall drew attention to several flag- rant cases of disobedience of the law amongst the list of defaulters—parents who were not sending their children to school • and asked for informatian why these persons had not been summoned before the magistrates.—Dr. Gastaldi, chairman of the Committee,said it was thought under the circumstances, that admonition would lead to better effects than compulsion in these cases, as the parents promised to send their children in future. —Mr. Taylor said he failed to see any necessity for a vote of censure on the Committee for the duties they had performed during the past month, as they had a better average than in June last year. If Dr. MacDougall, was anxious to see those people taken to the Town Halkas criminals, he was not, and he di t not intend to be a party to it because he thought they could do more in various other ways than summoning the parents.—Dr. MacDougall repelled the insinuation that he delighted in punishing the parents unnecessarily. Mr. Taylor had used his name in an unwarrantable manner, as he had a perfect right to make any inquiry he thought fit on this subject, but he was not going to follow Mr. Taylor's example and indulge in sentimental talk. He presumed the remark as to wholesale summons was a censure on the preceding Board, which no doubt had not the experience of Mr. Taylor, but they adopted such methods as appeared to them best, and he was sure that Mr. Pritchard would not be a party to the wholesale issuing of summonses. They were there to administer an Act of Parliament, and it was the duty of,the Com- mittee to deal with the matter not from a sentimental but a practical and technical point of view.—Dr. Gastaldi asked whether Dr. MacDougall condemned or approved of the work of the Committee during the past month.—Dr. MacDougall said he did neither.—Dr. Gastaldi said he must do one thing or another.—Mr. Taylor spoke of the difficulty of dealing with boat children, of which they had a large number in Runcorn,and it was a wonder to him that the per centage of attendance was so good.—The Clerk said the cases mentioned by Dr. MacDougall had been ordered to be summoned if the attendance was not better.—The Chairman pointed out that the Committee had only been in existence a month, and it was too early to judge of the results of the methods the Committee had adopted.—Dr. MacDougall • * agreed, but added that he should not relinquish his right to criticise.—Mr. Salkeld said it ought not to go forth to the public that the Board did not intend to summon people if they did not send their children to school.,--Dr. "Gastaldi said it was never entertained by the Committee for one moment that no parents should be summoned in future. He should always be in favour of extreme measures when required.—Mr. Taylor said the results showed there was more encouragement to adopt the course of the Committee during the past month, than to summon people.—Dr. MacDougall: You cannot take credit for the result that has taken place.—The Chair- man : It will be shown in next report.—Mr. taylor And I think it would have been better to have left the vote of censure until later.—Dr. MacDougall There is no vote of censure. There is criticism, and we have every right to criticise.—The discussion then ceased. PROPOSED SCIENCE AND ART CLASSES, &C. I . .

The School Management Committee reported that they I had taken into consideration the question of establishing I Science and Art Classes and a Continuation Evening School under the provisions of the Technical Instruction Act, 1889, and the Revised Code pf the year, and it the opinion of the Committee that the Board should offer to the public the advantages of the legislation "referred to, having due regard to the requirements of the dis- trict. The Committee suggest the establishment ot an Elementary Evening School at the Greenway-road Board School. This would afford an opportunity for scholars who have not attained a high standard in the day,schools to continue their education, and those scholars who have passed the sth standard could either proceed to the 6th or 7th standards, or confine themselves to specific sub- jects such as algebra, euclid, French, chemistry, book- keeping, shorthand, Etc. The Committee also suggest the establishment of an Art Class, as it is probable that such a class would be useful to elementary school teachers, now that drawing is a compulsory subject for boys. It was further suggested that the Clerk be authorised to 'advertise these educational facilities, ar4 to ascertain in the best way he can in what branches of instruction it was likely that classes can be formed, as a guide to the Board in the engagement of duly qualified teachers.— The minute was referred back on the motion of Dr. MacDougall, seconded by Dr. Gastaldi.

THE EFFECT OF A GOOD DINNER.

Mr. W. Whitlow gave notice that at the next meeting of the Board, he should move that the price of the din- ners provided in the House for the Guardians be raised from is. to 2s. each. He attended the Board a fortnight i ago, and the dinner that was laid before him was sump- tuous. He made a memorandum of what the cost would be and he found that there was a loss of 14s. on the dinner (laughter).—He had the memorandum and he would read it.—The Chairman: Hadn't you better give it next Board day.—Mr. Taylor: Let him give it now —Mr. Brandreth said the price of the meat was less than that paid by Mr. Whitlow for his own use. Mr. Whitlow: SOM3 of the meat was supplied by me first hand.—Mr. Brandreth : That is not so.—Mr. Whitlow: I will read the menu. A forequarter of lamb 10s., a couple of fowls 58., vegetables 2s. fld., two puddings and a tart 35.; bread, cheese and beer, 2s. Bd. ; attendance, is. 6d.; total £1 4s. Bd. The amount received from the Guar- dians was 10s —he collected the money himself (laughter)—and there was therefore a loss of 14s. Bd. on the dinner or £l9 is. 4d. a year, if this was an average dinner.—Mr. Speakman: During all the time I have come to the Board I never knew such an exorbitant dinner. It was specially got for our friend's benefit when he came. (Laughter.)—Mr. Whitlow: I rather think there was some object in view with that dinner. (Laughter.) The nearest way to an Englishman's heart is through his stomach, and that was the very day the matron was applying for an increase of salary. (Laughter.) And from the very elabOrate and flatter- ing remarks we had on our matron round the table, I am sure that dinner had very great effect. (Hear, hear and laughter.) There was, no doubt, some meaning in that dinner. (Renewed laughter.)—Mr. Taylor: I am sorry that dinner had not a better effect upon Mr. Whitlow. He seemed to enjoy it im- mensely; I don't know that there was one who enjoyed his dinner so Much. (Laughter.) I hope next time he comes he will even enjoy his dinner better.—Mr. Whit- low: I did enjoy my dinner' but I could not go away without the cmsciousness that I had been eating the ratepayers' money. (Laughter.)—Mr. Taylor: He did did not pay 25., Mr. Chairman. (Loud laughter.)—The Chairman : Do you wish notice given to each Guardian of your resolution fe—Mr. Whitlow Yes, please, The Board then dealt with the relief business.

A WOUNDING AFFRAY AT _RUNCORN.

_ At Cheshire Quarter Sessions, on Wednesday, Thomas O'Brien, 31, labourer, was charged with unlawfully and maliciously wounding john Hurley, of Canal-st., Run- corn, on May 29th. Mr. Colt-Williams prosecuted. The parties were in the Barley Mow public-house, in the even- in. when the prisoner took up a bottle, struck Hurley f" with it across the forehead, and, inflicted wounds that necessitated his going to Dr. Clifford, who attended to I the injuries There were five wounds, one of which was a quarter of an inch deep.' None of them he considered daugerous. Mr. McWilliams, the landlord of the inn said that the conversation in which Hurley and a friend were engaged might have offended O'Brien.—The Chair- man: What was the subject under discussion ?—Wit- ness :‘ They were conversing about Darwin's theory. (Laughter.)—The Chairman,: Did he think he was the missing link? (Renewed laughter.)—WitKss : He might think he'was like a monkey. (Roars of laughter.) When apprehended the prisoner said prosecutor struck him with a glass, and what he did was in self-defence. The jury recorded a: verdict of guilty, but recommended the prisoner to the clemency of the court. 'the Chairman said O'Brien had already undergone five weeks' imprison- ment, and therefore they would give him one more day, which meant that he could go at once.

THE NEW RECTOR op WINWICK. The late Vicar of Huyton, now Rector of Win- wick, was presented on Thursday night by the parishioners of Huyton Quarry. at their mission room, with a handsome study chair and stool of oak, upholstered in plush. The gift was feelingly acknowledged by Canon Penrhyn, who is to preach his farewell sermons on Sunday prior to his removal to Winwick.

ACCIDENT ON THE SHIP CANAL. On Monday a young man named IVilliam Lovatt, 25 years of age, was admitted into the Ship Canal Hospital at Latchford, suffering from injuries he had sustained wlailst following his occupation near Runcorn, the same day. It appears that whilst in the act of coupling some wagons he /was knocked down, and one of the trucks rolled across his legs, the result being that one of them was very badly broken and the other seriously cut.

THE SHIP CANAL AND TER CORN TRADE. A party of gentlemen interested in the corn trade 'visited the Manchester Ship Canal. on Wednesday, by invitation, and were conducted over the works by Mr. Marshall Stevens and Mr. J. K. Bythell (chairman of the Finance Committee of the Board of Directors), as representing the Company. The party travelled by ordinary train to Liverpool and crossed the Mersey to Eastham, where an inspection was first made of the locks and other works in pro- gres. From that point a journey was made along the course of the Canal to Manchester by the con- tractors' railway, refreshments being served en route. GARDEN TOOLS.—J. S. Garland, Ironmonger, Warrlagton Earlestown.

• FINED. At the Prescot Petty Sessions, on Tuesday, Win. Allen Citrtledge, described as a driver, of Liverpool, was charged with being drunk in charge of three horses and a mail coach at Prescot, on Saturday night. Police Constable Beckett deposed that the prisoner arrived at Prescot about eleven o'clock on Saturday night, and he was the driver of the Man- chester and Liverpool mail coach. Witness drew the attention of the Prescot stableman to his condition and said he would be detained, and he (the stable- man) must go on with the mails. Prisoner refused to be detained, however, and again mounted his box. He drove off for about 100 yards and another con- stable then took hold c>f the horses heads. The prisoner, who acknowledged that he was a little worse for liquor, was fined ss. and costs. UsuniEmas RE-COvERED On the premises at S. it W. Dutton's, Sankey-st.—Advt.

TB ALLEGED ATTEMPTED IlifliDEß OP A STEP

FATERn. At the St. Helens Town Hall, on Monday, before the Mayor (Alderman C. J. Bishop) andiAlderman 3. Harrison,•Dennis James Woods, a young man re- siding in Nook-lane, Sutton, was brought up on re- mand,on a charge of attempting to murder his step- father, James Oates, with an axe, early on the 22nd ult. Chief Constable Wood stated that the prisoner was charged with attempting to murder his step- father by striking him two severe blows, one on each side of the head, with an axe. Immediately afterwards the priker went and gave himself up to Sergeant Brooks. The sergeantwent with him and found that Oates was suffering from two large wounds on the head, and the prisoner was locked up and brought before the magistrates on the pre- vious Monday, when he was remanded until that day. The injured man had been confined to the house ever since, and was speechless for six days. His speech had returned on Sunday, but he would not be able to appear for another week. Sergeant Brooks repeated his evidence, which was given in our last issue, after which the Bench stated that the prisoner would be remanded tor another week. —Prisoner: Can I have bail ?—The Mayor: Cer- tainly not. WIIITECROSS COMPANY, LIMITED, V. CREDIT

LYONNAIS.

In the Queen's Bench Division, London, on Sabur- day, the Divisional Court, composed of Mr. Justice Denman, Mr. Justice Charles, and Mr. Justice Vaughan Williams, gave judgment in this appeal. Mr. Gully, Q.C., and Mr. B. Abrahams were the counsel for the Credit Lyonnais. The Whitecross Company were represented by Mr. H. T. Reid, and Mr. Hollams. The plaintiffs are manufacturers of iron and steel wire at Warrington; and they sued the Credit Lyonnais, the well-known bankers in Paris, who also have a branch at Madrid, to recover £1,446. The Whitecross Company entered into a contract, through their agents, Hamilton and Co., of Madrid, for the supply of a large quantity of tele- graph wire to the Spanish Government. They said they employed the defendants to get a transfer of the contract from the names of Hamilton and Co., to give the proper notice to the Spanish Govern- ment, and obtain payment either direct to the plain- tiffs or to themselves, on behalf of the plaintiffs. They complained that the defendants had not per- formed their duty, and the money had been paid to Hamilton and Co., and lost to the plaintiffs in con- sequence. The action was tried beforo Mr. Justice A. L. Smith and a special jury when the plaintiffs obtained a judgment for .2r,446.—T,heir lordships now gave judgment, dismissing the appeal with costs. PRESCOT BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The fortnightly meeting of the Prescot Board of Guar- dians was held at the Union Workhouse, Whiston, on Thursday afternoon. Major J. W. Wareing occupied the chair,there were also present Mr. F. Stapleton-Bretherton, J.P.' ex-officio; Rev. Dean Finegan, Messrs. W. Tyrer, C. B. Johnson, W. Middiehurst, J. Marsh, J. Burchall, J. Davies, J. Peover, W. Lowe, E. Syder, J. Goldsworth, J. Ranson, W. Aline. J. Kenwright,J. Birchall,J. Buckley, and S. H. Hartley (clerk.) GIFT OF ROC:MS.—The Master of the Workhouse I reported with thanks, the receipt of a quantity of books, periodicals and pictures,from Mrs. W. Tyrer, Woodleigh, Prescot, for the use of the inmates. RELIEF.—The Workhouse Master reported there were 476 inmates of the workhouse this week; 467 last week, and 471 in the corresponding week last year; 42 vagrants had been relieved during the week against 53 last year. THE Cognrriow OF IHRECILES.—The Local Govern- ment Board forwarded a copy of a report received by them from Mr. C. S. Bagot, one of the visiting Commis- sioners in Lunacy who had visited the Prescot Work- house on the 23rd ult. Mr. Ba.got wrote that he found 79 inmates, of whom 30 were males and 49 females, classed as lunatics, and he found their detention duly authorised. He was satisfied with the personal con- dition and the dress of the imbeciles of both sexes, and they seemed to be properly cared for. The accommoda- tion provided was adequate and comfortable. THE &PPLICATIONS FOR INCREASED SALARIES.—The minutes of the Finance Committee contained the follow- ing recommendation: That the applications from Miss Barton, organist, and Mr. E. Baxter, assistant overseer, be not entertained; and the further consideration of Mr. Kitchen's and Mr. Twist's, postponed for three months. THE MAINTENANCE OF PARENTS.—The Clerk read a copy of a petition from Paddington Union, in favour of the Maintenance of Destitute Parents Bill. He explained that the purport of the Bill was to enable destitute parents to take proceedings against their children who were able to contribute towards their support without first applying for parish relief .—The Board passed a resolution in sup- port of the measure, and the Clerk was instructed to send copies to local members of Parliament. THE FIRE BRIGADE.—Mr. Wm. Swift, clerk to the Huy- ton Local Board, wrote asking upon what terms and conditions the Union Fire Brigade would be permitted to attend any fire in the district. The Clerk was instructed to send a copy of the terms agreed upon by the Guar- dians in 1867. This was the principal business.

Barrow has been the burden of every" cry in Runcorn during the past week; in ...44t bye-election for for years past has created so much ex- citement in local circles. Amongst Liberals desire placed Mr. Duncan at the head of the poll, judgment and calculation pointed out Mr. Wainwright as the victor, for as the day of battle approached it became apparent from Mr. Caine's utterances, that with all his bumptious display of confidence, he felt himself in desperate straits. Arrangements had been made to have the result of the election telegraphed to the Liberal Club which was thronged with members and friends, while a large crowd awaited the intelli- gence outside. When the news of the great and unexpected victory arrived a tremendous shout of jubilation was raised both inside and out. An enthusiastic follower of the G. 0. M., whilst riding home by train, hours before the poll was closed, penned the following lines which now appear are quite prophetical :--

To Damw Duncan went to win, Hurrah! Hurrah! Tho c,rV vote he made it spin, Hurrah! Hurrah ! The Tory's out, the Howe'er they cry, or make a So we all feel gay now Duncan's Gone marching in. A worthy son of a worthy sire, Hurrah ! Hurrah! No wonder that he raised their ire, Hurrah! Hurrah! A man of pluck, a man of fire, ' Fair square, all round, hub, spoke and tyre. So we all feel gay now Duncan's Gone marching in. Then here's a health to our Great Chief, Hurrah ! Hurrah! To-day will yield him much relief, Hurrah ! Hurrah ! The Tories it will fill with grief, And make their short life still more brief. So we all feel gay now Duncan's Gone marching in. Runcorn Board of Guardians, if not famed in song and story, was about ten years ago the subject of pantomine jokes in regard to a certain poor little pony, and the potatoes found in its manger, which some of the members of that day thought was feed- ing a brute with what was meant for the paupers. The feeding of the Guardians themselves is to come under review at the next meeting on a motion made by Mr. Whitlow to increase the price of the dinners supplied from Is. to 2s. Mr. ;Whitlow alleges that at present there is a loss on the " sumptuous" meal provided, ;int no doubt the master will better be able to answer that statement. However it is absolutely necessary that dinner should be pro- vided at a reasonable rate, as many of the members have to be away from home from ten o'clock until four and often later and there is not a public-house within a mile where they can get a decent " snack." Surely the Gurdians have not to starve whilst attending to the ratepayers' business.

PENKETII. SUNDAY _SCHOOL TEACHERS' TRIP.

On Saturday, the annual trip of the teachers in connection avith the Wesleyan Sunday School took place. It having been decided that the trip this year should go to the "Children's Home," Edg- worth, near Bolton, a party numbering 23 teachers and friends left Fiddler's Ferry at 9.2 and Warring- ton (Bank Quay) at 9.19 a.m., en route for Bolton, which place was reached about 10.45 a.m. As Edgworth is some eight miles distant from Bolton, arrangements had been made to drive there. At the" Children's Home," through the kindness of the governor, Mr. Mager, about two hours were spent in examining the different homes and in seeing the work which is being done by Dr. Stephenson among the orphan and forsaken children. At 3.45 an excel- lent tea was served by the friends in. connectinn , with the home. Various games were indulged .in during the afternoon, and despite the rain which fell at different times., a very enjoyable day was spent. At 5.30 p.m. the return drive was com- menced, and Bolton reached at .about 6.20.. At 7.25 p.m. the train left for Warrington, which was reached about 9.30 p.m. INQUEST AT Kanrixsonoier. On Tuesday afternoon Mr. S. Brighouse, coroner for South-west Lancashire, held an inquest at Martinscroft regarding the death of a railway pensioner named Hamblet Taylor, who was 84 years of age, and who lived at Martinscroft. On the 7th of June thedeceased was chasing some fowls, when he fell and , hurt his thigh and side. He was attended by Dr. Fox, but died on Sunday. The jury found that death resulted from injuries caused by the fall.

RECONCILED,

Ambrose Fotlea was before the Warrington magistrates on Wednesday, on a charge of having assaulted his wife. The offence happened last year, and Foden, who then absconded, had surrendered. His wife now told the magistrates that she had been living with him at Sheffield since Christmas, and he. had been very good to her from that time to ,the present. Under these circumstances the case was dismissed on payment of ss. costs. A Sr. HELENS MAN'S FIDE IN A HAND-CART. George Parr, a St. Helens glass worker, was brought , before the Warrington magistrates on Monday on a charge of drunkenness in St. Austen's-lane. Evidence was given that he was taken to tho Bridewell in a hand- cart, and Alderman Harrison remarked that he did not seem grateful to the officers for having taken care of him, and given him a ride for nettling. Prisoner : I do not know who brought me. It was his first appearance,and the Bench decided to dismiss him. THE SCAPEGRACE OF THE FAMILY.

At the Warrington Police Court, on Monday, Bernard Mannion, a youth, was charged with having assaulted his sister on the 21st ult. It was stated in evidence that prisoner, not being pleased with his dinner, threw it on the floor, which had just been cleaned. He then assaulted his sister, and in the evening began smashing some of the windows of the house. The Mayor told prisoner he should be ashamed of himself, and sent him to prison for a month, with hard labour.

AN APPLETON STABLEMAN'S COSTLY SPREE.

. _ Thomas Daley. a stableman from Appleton, had a spree on Tuesday evening, for which he was called upon to pay dearly next morning brthe Warrington magistrates. In addition to having to answer for drunkenness and disorderly conduct, there was a charge against him of having smashed a Otte-glass window at the Crown and Sceptre Inn, Buttermarket-at. For the first offence he was ordered to pay a fine of 10s. and costs, or go to prison for a fortnight, and in regard to the second charge the decision was that he should pay £3 as damages, or serve in prison for a month longer.

AN INCORRIGIBLE GIRL..

.... The Warrington magistrates, for the second time within a week. on Monday had before them a young girl named Jane Waterworth, who is apparently of rather weak intellect. On Saturday morning she was arrested in Buttermarket-st., for being drunk and disorderly, that also being the offence with which she was charged on the previous Thursday. On the latter occasion she was excused on her promise to go to the Workhouse, but when a constable took her there she refused to redeem her promise. The magistrates, deeming her incorrigible, intimated that they felt they had no choice but to send her to prison for a fortnight, with hard labour.

ROBBING A NAVVY OF His _PURSE.

A Ship Canal navvy, named Joseph Starkey appeared at the Warrington Police Court, on Monday morning, to prefer a charge against a young woman of bad charac- ter, aamed Elizabeth Ellen Fleming, whom he accused of having stolen hispurse, whilst in the King's Arms Inn, Buttermarket-st., on Saturday evening. The purse con- tained a sixpenny piece. Complainant alleged thatwhen sitting in the house, prisoner picked his pocket of the purse, and he at once went out and informed Constable Bell of what had occurred. Prisoner at first denied hav- ing taken the purse, but afterwards she showed the con- stable where she had hidden it, and asked him not to make a case of it. When charged at the Police Station she replied, "I shall never have any luck in Warrington.' She was now sent to prison for a fortnight. ANY PORT IN A STORII.

Wm. Gallagher, of the labouring class made a conjec- ture on Saturday evening, the result of which proved that although he was drunk he still had some notion of what he was about. Having got more than was at alt convenient for him in the shape of liquor, cr, as he put it when before the magistrates on Monday, "having had a drop taken," he sagely came to the conclusion that if he would be safe anywhere it would be at the Bride- well. Thither he went, and though an officer told him he could go home and have a sleep, he persisted in i remaining n his refuge now that he had found it. He was kept safe until Monday, when he was charged. It happened to be his eleventh appearance, but as he was one of those who had "never yet been forgiven," the Bench let him off, as much to his surprise as his relief. AM UNFORTUNATE MAN WITH AN UNFORTUNATE NAME. The Warrington magistrates had before them on Monday a man named WilliamCaskey,the charge entered against him being that of drunkenness and disorderly conduct. He was drunk in Buttermarket-st., on Satur- day night, and refused to walk home. A constable pro- cured a cab for him, and prisoner paid the fare, but ultimately he refused to use the cab. As he was so hard to please, the constable locked him up. Prisoner said he just came into the town with a basket, a,nd got two or three glasses of drink. He had been "in" since Satur- day. He was an "awful man for misfortune," (Laughter.) Alderman Davies: You are unfortunate your naro e—" Cask "-ey. (Laughter.) Prisoner remarked that Alderman Harrison knew that he was a hard- working man, but that gentleman disclaimed any knowledge of him. A fine of 10s. and costs was imposed.

A" PROPErt " MIZTY WOMAN.

At the Warrington Police Court, on Monday, James McKean was summoned for overcrowding his house. Inspector Norman said that 'defendant was summoned for keeping a house in Naylor-street in a dirty condition, but as soon as the summons arrived, he left the place and it was some time before he could be traced. He was at length found living with his wife and four child- ren in a house in James-street, which was adapted for only two persons. The wife was a" proper" dirty woman. Alderman Harrison : A " proper " dirty woman ? What do you mean by that ? I should call her an improperly dirty woman. Inspector Norman : I believe it is the proper term. (Laughter.) I don't think she washes her children from week's end to week's end. They are simply lost in dirt. Defendant did not appear, and the Bench made an order upon him to pay the costs.

A SERIES OF THEFTS BY A GIRL. On Thursday, at the Warrington Police Court, a girl named Ellen Aplin (10), daughter of a Ship Canal labour- er, living in Knutsford-road, was charged with having stOlen a number of articles from various places during the past few days. The mother of the girl was charged with having received some of the articles knowing them to have been stolen. It appeared that on Wednesday evening the girl was detected stealing a brush and a money box from the stall of Samuel Devonport, small- ware dealer, Market-place, and was afterwards taken into custody. On a search being made at her home, four pairs of shoes, and other articles, were found, and the younger prisoner now admitted having stolen them from the shop of James West, draper, Market-place ; J. Newman. boot and shoe dealer, Bridge-st. ; and the Staffordshire Boot and Shoe Company. Mrs. Aplin said that her daughter brought the things home, and said that she found them. The Chairman (Major Litton), said that they thought the mother greatly to blame, but in consideraaion of her young family they would only fine her 10s. and costs, and record a conviction. In the case of the girl a conviction was recorded. ALLEGED ASSAULT IN A PENIEETH PUBLIC-HOUSE. On Wednesday morning, at the Warrington County Petty Sessions, John Townley was summoned for having, on the 22nd ult., been disorderly on the premises of Eliza Worrall, the Red Lion Inn, Penketh, on being requested to leave ; and for having assaulted Thomas Williams, the barman. Another man named George Kenwright was also summoned for assaulting Williams, who, in his turn, was summoned for having assaulted Townley. The cases were taken together, Mr. P. Davies appearing for Williams, and Mr. A. Browne for Townley and Ken- wright. It was alleged by the barman Williams that on the night in question, a Sunday, Townley and Ken- wright were in the house. Townley made use of bad language' and refused to leave the house. Atterwards he struck Williams on the head and face, causing blood to flow. It was further alleged that Kenwright particip- ated in the assault by holding Williams while Townley struck him. Williams was rendered unconscious, and had to be medically attended for several days The defence was that Williams commenced a quarrel with Townley, that the two fought, and that all that Ken- wright did was to endeavour to separate the combatants. The barman, Mr. Browne alleged, was the one to give the first blow. The case occupied a long time in hearing, and at the conclusion the Bench fined Townley 10s. and costs for the assault on Williams, and dismissed all the other charges. --

A VIIRY MISERABLE MARRIED LIFE. At the Warrington Police Court, on Tuesday morning, a man named William Bibby, of 12, Appleton-st., was summoned for having used threats towards his wife on the 29th ult, and having assaulted her on the night of the previous Thursday. and on subssluent nights. There was a cross-summons charging the wife with having assaulted her husband. Mr. A. Browne, who appeared for Mrs. Bibby, gave her version of the matter, and drew a pitiable picture. Ile said that she was constantly subjected to ill-treatment at the hands of her husband, who refused to give her money for food or clothes. She earned some money on Walking Day week and because she would not give it to her husband he said that he would take her life, or have her earnings. The same night he burned and destroyed several articles of her attire, in order that she should not go out on walking Day. On the night of that day he used bad language, struck her in the mouth, and made her sleep without bedclothes. Mrs. Bibby said that she as married to the defendant in 1888. They had had one child, which died about a fortnight ago. She described her alleged treatment by her husband, and said that he threatened to murder her if she did not accept 2s. 6d. a week, leave him. He said—" I'll be the end of you; I mean it, so help me God." She was afraid to live with him. Defendant averred that his wife would not get his meals ready, and that she insulted him. lle had never said a wrong word to her. She had threatened to cut his throat if he went to sleep. Tie had done his best, and when she was confined waited on her hand and foot. She had said that she would be " with Mrs. Maybrick " in a very few months. After evidence had been heard on both sides, the magistrates said they thought it would be the wisest plan for the two to be •separated. Acting on the suggestion, Bibby undertook to pay his wi-ife ss. a week, and not to molest her in any way.

GIVING THEM ANOTHER CHANCE.

Mary Knockton and Ellen Hart were before the War- rington magistrates on Monday for drunkenness. Knock- ton Is 27 years of age, and it•was her 20th appearance. Alderman Davies: It would require wiser men than we to know what to do with these girls. It transpired that Knockton had only been excused once, and the Bench, in the hope that leniency might do some good, dizmissed the girls.

TEE PROTECTFON OF BANE PARE GARDENS. At the Borough Court, yesterday, two lads named James Herbert Eastwood and William Sharpies, were summoned for disorderly behaviour in Bank Park Gar- dens on the 29th June. Mr. Ashton, the Park keeper, stated that on the Sunday evening in question one of the defendants was climbing behind one of the seats, when the other defendant pushed him down, and a branch of one of the bushes was broken. Considerable annoyance was caused by such disorderly behaviour in the gardens. Chief Constable Hunt said the Park Committee had at- tempted to deal with the annoyance by cautioning the offenders, but they found it had no effect, and they had decided that the magistrates should be asked to deal with them in future. The Bench said they would be lenient in this case, but warned the defendants about their future conduct.—Each boy was fined 2s. 6d.

A VIOLENT LOVER IN COURT.

At the Born-uili--&-rt-,--yest-erCiay-,- ip-e-iOre Mr. John White and other magistrates, Patrick Hoarty, a young man,was summoned for assaulting a young woman nam- ed Emma Stretton' on the 30th June. P.C. Hughes stated that on the date named he saw the defendant pursuing the complainant past the police station: The defendant overtook her, seized her by the throat and kicked her several times. The complainant on being called to give evidence, said that the defendant had sev- eral times promised to marry her. She did not wish to press the case if the defendant would marry her. It was stated that the parties lived together and the defendant added that he struck the woman because she would not mend his trousers.—The Chairman: Will you marry her? Defendant: Yes, sir. The Bench bound the defend- ant over to keep the peace on the condition that he married the complainant, the Chairman remarking that if he did net marry her the penalty would be more severe.

AN IRrsTt Row.

James Hogan, resident in a court off Fennel-st., was summoned for assaulting Bartley Loyden, a neighbour. There was a summons issued by Hogan against Sarah Loyden, wife of the complainant in the former case, for an assault alleged to have been committed at the same time. The evidence showed that there had been some

ill-feeling between the parties, and it was alleged that Hogan knocked Loyden down and knelt on his breast. Mrs. Loyden thereupon rushed out of the house with a poker and struck Hogan a blow on the head, from which blood flowed profusely. Hogan's head had to be band- aged at the Infirmary. Mrs. Loyden, with great volubi- lity, explained to the Bench that Hogan wanted to "slaughter and murder the lot of them." and she only went to the protection of her "old man." She gave Hogan a" crack" on,the head, and saved her old man's life. (Laughter.) The Bench ordered Mrs. Loyden to be bound over to keep the peace, and dismissed the case against Hogan.

INSANITARY DWELLINGS.

John Appleton, a builder, of Orford-avenue, was sum- moned for allowing nuisances to exist on property in Dallam-lane.—Mr. Forshaw appeared for the defendant, and stated that the work was being done this week. It would have been completed before but for the interven- tion of Walking Day. He. therefore, asked for an ad- journment of the summons until Monday next.— Inspector Norman said that the houses were number 67, 69, 71, and 73. Dallam-lane. On the 18th June he inspected the premises, and found the drains blocked up, and spouts in a defective condition, and underneath the parlour floor he discovered six inches depth of sewage water.—Dr. Gornall medical officer of health, said that he had certified the houses as unfit for human habitation, ' and the Committee had sanctioned an order for their closure in case Mr. Appleton did not carry out their directions thoroughly.—Mr. Forshaw repeated that the work would be done this week and the case was adjourned.

A CONSTABLE SCTAIMONED FOR ASSABLT.

Yesterday, at the police court, Police Constable Wad 3, residing at 5, Porter-st., was summoned for assaulting a married woman named Julia Melbourne,who stated that on the 25th June she was standing at the corner of Porter and Napier-streets, when the officer came up and told her to go away. She asked who he was speaking to and he replied "You dirty low woman go away. He afterwards struck her on the left breast and pushed her. Mrs. Ellison, Miss Dagnall, and a Mrs. Higginson gave corroborative testimony. The defendant stated that for some time past he had been considerably annoyed by the crowds of noisy children who had shouted and created a disturbance when he was in bed before going on night duty. On the night in question his wife threw some water upon them to drive them away, and about 200 women gathered in the street and created a disturbance. They declined to move on and he had to clear the street. He used no more violence than was necessary.—Several witnesses were called in support of the officer's statement and the case was dis- missed.

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

CRICKET. The writer Of cricket notes in the " Manchester Guardian" makes same pertinent remarks on the practice of hiring county professionals to take part in district "club" matches. No attempt, he says, has been made on the part of the professionals to defend the practice except that it teaches the country clubs to play better cricket. "'We have no objection—quite the contrary— to the county clubs engaging the beat possible talent to coach them; but we do most strongly object to a club taking men to matches who are not bona-fide members of 'its organisation, and thereby obtaining an unfair advant- age over clubs which are more scrupulous or less wealthy. Let each club play" lair ana square" on its own merits, and then managers will know what, and with whom, they are dealing in making matches. A colliery district not far from Bolton boast's a rather powerful club, but not one of its members lives in the place covered by the title. They are professionals pure and simple from a distance, and yet they are supposed to represent the cricketing talent of that particular district.

We are told that the "three centuries " in one innings at cricket were made thrice in 1884; and Wisden's Almanack for 1888 shows that it occurred twice in 1887. In that year, in the Notts v Sussex match, Shrewsbury scored 135, Gunn 205 (not out), and Barnes 120—total of the innings, 570. In the Canterbury Week match between Yorkshire and Kent Ulyett scored 124, Hall 110, and Lee 119—total of the innings 559.

===== 0002601_18900705_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
MISCELLANEOUS.

H. H. Evans, of Warrington, was successful at the Accrington sports in carrying off two prizes, both seconds, in the 120 and 220 yards handicaps respectively. In the 120 yards race he was accorded 11i yards start, whilst in the22o run he was conceded 15 yards.

The athletes of Widnes had a fairly good time on Satur- day. At the Accrington Cricket Club Sports, J. Kelly with 70 yards start,won the mile handicap by two yards from P. J. Kenyon, also a Widnesian with 115 yards start. In a race over a similar distance at Pendleton, H. Barber, who had 85 yards start, took the first prize, winning by eight yards.

It is said that baseball is already played by about 90 clubs in England, the great majority of whom are com- posed of amateurs. There is an " Amateur Association of Baseball Players" in the Newcastle district, and the game has been taken up in Glasgow. We have already, in fact, a baseball championship, contested by the "Na- tional League of Professional Baseball Clubs of Great Britain." There are four members—namely, Preston North End, Derby, Birmingh6m (Aston Villa), and Stoke,—of which Derby has been hitherto the most suc- cessful ; and baseballers confidently hope to see the day when cricket, with its two or three days' slow progres- sion, will give place to the two hours' keen excitement of "catching," "pitching," and "base running." Umpires, of course, will need to be heavily insured or armour plated, but that is a detail which may be ar- ranged.

===== 0002601_18900705_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
THE BARROW ELECTION.

GREAT LIBERAL VICTORY. There was great excitement at Barrow on Wednesday,l the day of the polling. During the day Mr. Caino took things easy, but the Liberals and the Conservatives both worked very hard. Amongst other workers for Mr. Duncan, were Messrs. W. O'Brien, M.P., J. Wilson,M.P., T. Harrington, M.P.,and Mr. Conway, M-P.,with Fathers Caffrey and Fitzgerald, and two Irish ladies. For the Conservative candidate there assembled quite a host of workers, amongst the number being several Primrose League dames. At the close of the poll, the ballot boxes were taken to the old Town Hall, where they were counted in the presence of the returning officer (the Mayor), and the three candidates. A large crowd as- sembled in the open space during the process of count- ing, and when the result was announced as follows a loud cheer was raised by the victolious voters : Mr. Duncan (L) Mr. Wainwright-(C) Mr. Caine (I L) After the ,figures had been announced Mr. Duncan drove to his committee-rooms, where he was received with a great outburst of enthusiasm. The cheering with which he was received was continued for about twenty minutes. In a short address he spoke of the verdict of Barrow as a message of doom to the Government and of goodwill to Ireland. Amongst Liberals all ever the country the victory was received with great enthusiasm. Mr. Duncan and the Liberal Association of Barrow received many congratulatory telegrams. LETTER FROM MR. CAINE TO MR. DUNCAN. On returning to his hotel, immediately after the declar- ation of the poll, Mr. Caine sent the tollowing letter to the successful candidate .

"Dear Mr. Duncan,—Let me be among the first to send you my congratulations. lam thankful the seat has not gone to the representative of the liquor interest. I have nothing to complain of whatever in your conduct during the election, which has been that of a true gentleman, and a good Liberal. I have been beaten by a heavy combination, against which it has been impossible to stand up. I accept my defeat frankly, and whatever I can do in the future to reconcile the discordant elements of the Ljberal party in Barrow shall be done frankly and loyally.' lam glad, at any rate, that the seat has passed fr from me to the son of an old and valued friend, who is connected with Barrow by ties of affection and remem- brance in connection with great injustice. I heartily wish you a most successful career. Your constituency is the best in the country, and I am sure its interests and reputation will be safe in your hands.—With kindest re- gards, most sincerely yours, "W. S. CAINH."

THE UNITED KINGDOM_ ALLIANCE AND MR.

CAINE.

The United Kingdom Alliance London Committee, on Thursday evening, resolved—" That this Committee de- sire to express their cordial sympathy with Mr. Caine in the gallant contest which he has recently carried on at Barrow, and to concratulate him upon the considerable electoral support which under the difficulties of the situa- tion was accorded to him. The Committee hope that at an early day Mr. Caine will again occupy a seat in the House of Commons, and there renew those efforts on be- half of temperance legislation, which have so honourably distinguished his Parliamentary and public career."

MR. CAINE ON HIS DEFEAT. Mr. Caine was interviewed on his return to London ' from Barrow on Thursday. He stated that he intended going to India for two or three months in the winter, but he meant to get into Farliament again, and before long. "It is clear to my mind," he cmtinued, "that the Unionist party has had its chance and thrown it away. I vacated my. seat not so much because the compensation clauses were Introduced as that remedial legislation for Ireland was set aside for a measure foredoorned to certain failure. I can now only look to the main body of the Liberal party for Ireland's future, and shall, EO far as I:am able, co-operate with them in 'any at- tempt to settle the question." After remarking that his position in the contest was far better than he thought it would have been, Mr. Caine said, "The only matter that has given me real concern in the con- test is the report of a few ill-considered sentences about Mr. Gladstone, spoken by me in a heated moment in my committee-rooms when his letter to Mr. Duncan was brought in. We were, of course, greatly dismayed!' He admitted that his success was rendered impossible by the Liberal leaOer's letter adopting Mr. Duncan, and added that Sir Wlifrid Lawson's letter was a serious blow, and one that he did not expect. He expressed the belief that he would not have much difficulty in finding a 'constitu- ency, and said he would like to contest a constituency against one of his old colleagues, as that would emphasise his action. He hoped to attack a big brewer some- where. THE MARQUIS OF RIPON ON THE BARROW

- ELECTION. Speaking at a Liberal demonstration at Hutton Hall, near Middlesborough, on Thursday night, the Marquis of Ripon said that when he first heard that Mr. Caine had resigned his seat for Barrow he felt a desire that he might again be returned, because Mr. Caine had done good service in the last fight. He had stuck steadfastly and honestly to his temperance principles when some people did not stick to them, and tie should have been glad for many reasons if it had been possible for*. Caine to have been returned. That, however, was a matterthat rested with the people of Barrow themselves. What was the lesson to be learnt from the result ? There were many things to make one anxious that Mr. Caine should be again returned—consideration for his temperance principles, a feeling that he had thrown himself in a generous manner on the Puffra ges of the electors, and other reasons, but the electors of flarrow,he had no doubt,felt that the great question for the Liberal party to-day was to get rid of coercion in Ireland and to bring about the establishment of Home Rule. He felt they must have the Irish question out of the way before they could proceed with the social. reforms crowding upon them, and which they as ardent Liberals desired to see passed.

THE CHESHIRE POLICE INQUIRY.

MORE STARTLING CHARGES. - At Chester Castle, on Saturday morning, the coin-. mittee appointed by the standing joint Committee to in- quire into the allegations made against the Cheshire Constabulary by Mr. J. Slater Lewis, resumed their inquiry. His Honour Sir Horatio Lloyd (chairman), presided, and the other members of the Committee pre- sent were Mr. Duncan Graham, Mr. C. Lister, and Mr. J. Beckett. Mr. A. E. Fletcher, solicitor, dorthwich, attended and watched the proceedings on behalf of the police ; and there were also present the Chief Constable (Col. Hamersley), Superintendents Hollingsworth and Large, ex-Inspector O'Donnell, Mr. J. Slater Lewis Mr. E. Bte.ssey, solicitor, Chester (his adviser), Mr. J. Ashton (magistrates' clerk, Runcorn), and Mr. Reginald Potts (clerk of the County Council). Complying with a requisition of the committee at the last meeting, Mr. J. Slater Lewis had prepared a state- ment of the charges which he made against the police. They were as follows :—" General Accusations: Grave irregularities amongst the police at Runcorn from 1836 to 1889, consisting of drunkenness and other misconduct, much in excess of an ordinary proportion of such irre- gularities. (a) Personal charge against Superintendent Hollingsworth, being by his conduct and character an unfit man to have held the position of superintendent in that town. Aid of Game Preservation : 1. That the

police have from time to time, especially within the last fourteen years, been employed, to the neglect of their proper duty, in aiding gamekeepers at nights, some- times going on private land therefor, their beats being meanwhile totally unprotected from crime. 2. That it is part of the police system generally to conceal the fact of so doing by entries in their journals mislead- ink the Government Inspector of Police. (a) Specific Charge : That on the 24th of September, 1889, the town of Runcorn was cleared of all police on duty (ex-

. . cept office clerk) from 11 p.m. to 4 a.., or thereabouts, for the purpose of aiding keepers on Sir R. Brooke's es- tate at Norton, several miles from their ordinary beats, with the consent or knowledge of Supt. Large, and that their beats in Runcorn were meanwhile totally un- _ _ _ _ _ . .

guarded. Game Fund :3. That constables in and ibout the district of Macclesfield did formerly, if not now, re- ceive gratuities for every conviction of poachers ob- tained by them in the police courts, from some fund be- lieved to be established by game preservers in and about that district, some of whom were justices of the peace. Mr. J. Slater Lewis only takes upon himself the proof of such matters, but does not feel justified in usurping the committee's functions to the persons on whom the exact responsibility rests." Mr. Fletcher, on behalf of Colonel Hamersley, called attention to the nature of the allegations. He under- stood they were to have full particulars of the serious charges made by Mr. Lewis, and which were the subject of the inquiry. The document containing the particulars was really not stated, in the sense that it afforded them an opportunity of bringing any evidence to bear on the subjects. They were general statements which they had heard over and over again, and did not carry the matter one jot further than had already been indicated in the public papers and by Mr. Lewis in his opening. That list of documents which Mr. Lewis desired pioduced was intended to be supplemental to those very vague and general accusations. What Mr. Lewis proposed to do, it seemedto him, was to go on a sort of fishing expedition through the documents to see if he Could find something that would give colour to the charges. He apprehended that that was hardly the course that ought to be taken. The documents referred to consisted of duty books, and reports of a confidential character which had been made to the head office ; and obviously it would militate very ' much against the conduct of the force if the inquiry had the result of bringing up documents which were most privileged. So far as Colonel Hamersley was concerned, there was not the least objection to their production, but, holding the position as he did of chief constable, he felt that he had something more to considerthan his own personal wishes. He (the Colonel) feared that very much damage might result by an indiscriminate produc- tion of such documents. They proposed to produce documents through the Clerk, and then the Committee would see whether there was any ground for the allega- tions made, and which the documents were supposed to throw light npon. Mr. Lewis submitted that as they were to go into the charges, they were entitled to see the whole of the docu- ments ; and he respectfully asked the committee to order their production. Mr. Brassey said they had given notice to produce cer- tain documents and books, but they did not ask for them to be placed in their own handS. The documents were asked for the close purpose of proving or supporting their witnesses. For instance, constables and ex-constables who would be called should be shown their journals, in order that they might speak to particular dates. The Chairman remarked that the request was reason- able and in conformity with the procedure of all courts. Mr. Lewis then proceeded to call witnesses. Henry Cooper .stated that he resided at Runcorn, and had been a member of the Cheshire police force for over sixteen years. He had been stationed at Crewe before he was removed to Runcorn, where he was stationed about a year and a half. He was at Crewe under Superintendent Hollingsworth. During that time he saw him drunk frequently. On several occasions lie had seen him leave hotels at early hours in the morning, and especially one which was opposite the police station. It had been mentioned to him that a constable had seen Hollingsworth in one ,of the cells at Crewe where a woman had been locked up for drunkenness. The Chairman said he could not see that that could throw any light on the state of things at Runcorn. They felt that the matter should not be brought forward unless they were prepared to prove it. Mr. Brassey said they proposed to call the constable. The witness, continuing, ,said that when he went to Runcorn everything went on very well until Superin- tendent Hollingsworth went there. Hollingsworth com- menced to drink, and neglected his duty very much. He had reason to require his services frequently in Run- corn, and was obliged to find him. He gave evidence at i the inquiry held by the chief constable n 1887. Mr. Lewis then examined witness as to money known as vagrant money;, but the chairman considered it im- material to the inquiry. Witness, continuing, said he did not report the superin- tendent for being drunk, because there was an inspector over him. Cross-examined by Mr. Fletcker : At the inquiry in 1887 he did not state to the chief constable all he knew about Hollingsworth. Ile did not give all the informa- tion he knew, because he knew he would share the same fate as Sergeant Blair did. He was very careful not to go too i'ar at the inquiry in giving information. Could not say how many tines he had seen Hollingsworth drunk. Mr. J. Beckett : Did you not feel in your mind that there was a higher duty to the public and the position which you held than the one that made you shield a man that was evidently unfit for the position he held? Witness: Yes sir; but if you had been in the police force, and understood all the rples and regulations, you would hesitate a long time before you would have re- ported a supetior officer, because you might as well be out of the force, sir. (Laughter.) Mr. Fletcher: That is only Mr. Cooper's observa- tion. Mr. Slater Lewis: It is a very important observa- tion: '

Witness, resuming: That is the reason I did not tell Colonel Hamersley all, because I saw it was a one-sided investigation, and it proved to be so, because Sergeant Blair was reduced after the man had brought seven or eight constables up to prove the case. It was most monstrous. He was brought to the inquiry against his will, and dragged in it by two constables. He did not volunteer information and made no report, as he did not want to be mixed up in the affair, as he told Hollings- worth before he went into the room to give evi- dence. Joseph Wright, who was called, said he was an ex- policeman, having been in the Cheshire constabulary about 18 years. He Mad been stationed at Crewe under Inspector Hollingsworth, and he left the force through taking too much drink and assaulting a mart. He had seen Mr. Hollingsworth the worse fordrink at Crewe,but had not reported him for it At the time he saw Hollings- worth in the cell with the woman he was drunk. Hollingsworth was in the cell a few minutes with the woman. The Chairman remarked that if it was a true story it assumed important proportions, and Hollingsworth ought to have had notice that it was to be brought out. Mr. Brassey said they had, not seen the witness till that morning. Cross-examined by Mr. Fletcher: If they had reported Hollingsworth every time they had seen him drunk they would have been doing nothing else. (Laughter.) He had had to tell scores of lies to screen Hollingsworth, because thaw dared not say 'anything against a superior officer against his will. If he had done what Hollings- worth did when he (witness) was at Crewe, he would have been " stripped " long before he was. Hollings- worth was never fit to wear the clothes. Witness could not say how many 4,imes he was reported by the superin- tendent for being drunk. He (witness) was as fond of a glass of beer as Hollingsworth was. He had been fined. The reports made against superior officers went through their hands, and they could please themselves whether they forwarded them to the head office or not. The Chairman here read an extract from the police de- faulters' book, showing that Wright had been fined eleven times, chiefly for being drunk whilst en duty, disobedience of orders, and on one occasion for taking off his coat to fight ' • and after repeated reprimands and fines was discharged. Thomas Reece was called, and said he had been a member of the force for fourteen years, and left about' four years ago with a gocd character, and a gratuity was granted him. He knew Hollingsworth, and served un- der him at Crewe for some time. Ile spoke to seeing Supt. Hollingsworth coming several times into the police office intoxicated. When he left the force he wrote an anonymous letter to the chief constable, informing him that the head of the police in Crewe had been seen drunk in a public-house; that he was the last man to do another man an injury, but if the chief would go over to Crewe any night it would be an easy matter 14 catch him in that condition. Cros-examined.—He thought it was a great shame for a man to carry on as Hollingsworth did without the Chief Constable knowing of it.

to }and in his resignation in consequence of his interfer- ance with office work, and because • Superintendent Large said he could not possibly work with him. His intention was to reduce O'Donnell in rank, but before

butinformed O'Donnell, who visited him, that he mils; resign his appointment as inspector, and he should re- appoint him that day at £9O per annum. Police-constable J. That was when the superintendent, who had been visiting the sections, returned home and fell out of the trap. of police in that section was 39, and three only were reported for drunkenness during O'Donnell's sixteen months there.

Charles Potts, .sanitary .inspector, Northwich, was

The Chairman.—l cannot understand that you call a witness who everyone knows is a respectable man, and before asking questions you attempt to discredit him. Mr. Lewis.—l do not want to do that. it might come out in the course of the inquiry, and I want to prove that this statement is incorrect.
