Constable Billington, of Grappenhall, who had been three years in Runcorn, said he had seen Hollingsworth under the influence of drink. Constable Benfield, of Runcorn, said he had never seen _ . .

Hollingsworth drunk. Cross-examined.—O'Donnell had threatened to report -him for not cleaning his buttons. O'Donnell seemed to be a very harsh disciplinarian. Constable P. Hamersley, of Weston Point, said he was stationed in Runcorn, and on one occasion he thought Hollingsworth was drunk because his gait was hardly steady. 'He had, however, seen him come from an hotel during prohibited hours. Constable James, stationed near Nantwich, and who had been at Crewe under Hollingsworth, said he had never seen him under the influence of drink, and testified that he was a sober man. Constable William Potts, of Newton, near Hyde, said he was at Runcorn two years. He was reported for

getting a glass of whisky' when on duty, and fined 105... He had seen Hollingsworth under the influence of liquor. The Chairman said that he had received a resolution

from a public meeting, and said to have been carried by an overwhelming majority. It ran :—" That this meet- ing is of opinion that the money spent on this inquiry by i the Cheshire County Council s an absolute waste of public money, and totally unjustifiable." Mr. Lewis proposed that the resolution should be left on the table. He understood that it came from a publi- can. ' Mr. Peter Smith rose and denied that. He said the meeting was advertised, and the room was crowded with respectable people. There were only six dissentients. Mr. D. Graham said it was not a matter that they should take any notice of ; but still the fact remained that that inquiry was costing an enormous sum of money, and they must economise as far as possible time and money: The inquiry was then adjourned. • CONSTABLES AND THE PROTECTION OF GAME. The committee of inquiry appointed by the standing joint committee to investigate charges made against the administration of the Cheshire police by Mr. J. Slater Lewis sat for the fourth time at Chester Castle on Tues- day. His Honour Sir Horatio Lloyd, chairman of quarter sessions, presided, the other members of the Committee being Mr. Duncan Graham (chairman county council) and Mr. J. Beckett. Henry Lloyd was first called, and stated that he was in the Cheshire Constabulary prior to 1887, and was stationed at•Nevv Ferry, at Crewe, at Northwich, and at Sale. He deposed that he had seen Superintendent Hollingsworth drunk at Northwich,when driving a trap. —The Chairman, referring to witness's record, said that thosedocuments were important sometimes as to the value of the witnesses. Against that man there were no fewer than eight records MX of drunkenness—fined and reprimanded, disrated, fined and reprimanded, disrated, fined and reprimanded, and finally dismissed. Mr. Slater Lewis then intimated that he should take evidence as to fees having been received bythe police for taking poachers, the evidence to extend over a number of years.—Mr. Fletcher requested that hie inquiry should be limited to Col. Hamersley's administration, viz., from 1881. To take them back to people over whom they had no control could not do any good to the service or to the public. After a consultation with the committee, the Chair- man said they thought the limit proposed would be objectionable, because there was no suggestion in the charge that it was one against Colonel Hamersley, and they should be very sorry to give any colour that it was a personal accusation against Colonel Hamersley. The accusation was not against the police system as adminis- tered by Colonel Hamersley alone, but as administered for years past by whomsoever it might be. Hugh O'Donnell, ex-inspector, was then re-called, and stated that on the 24th September, 1889, he searched Church-st., and High-st.,Runcorn,and could find no con- stables. He then returned to the office, and searched the parade book to see where the men should be. The clerk, seeing him consulting the book and making a search, said " If you are looking for the men, they are all gone to Halton:" He further informed wftness that Sergeant Dutton, stationed then at Sutton Weaver, had sent to the superintendent; and.he had directed the sergeant to take the men off the town to meet some poachers suspected of coming to the Norton estate—Sir, Richard Brookes' place—that night. —Continuing, svitriess stated that at about twelve o'clock he reached Halton, which was between three and four miles from Runcorn. he thought. He there found the men close to the waterworks, some of them lying behind the hedge in a field, and others on the road with the sergeants. There were eleven keepers and watchers, some in the field, and others talking to the sergeants. Witness at once asked Sergeant Gosling who told him to go there. He said Superintendent Large had told him. Witness saw the thing was out of his hands, and thought the best thing to do would be to remain there. He insisted upon the men coming on to the highway, upon which the men left the field and remained on the road till morning.—By Mr. Beckett : Through the police going to Halton, Runcorn and Sutton Weaver would be left absolutely without any protection. On the fallowing morning witness reported to Superintend- ent Large on the matter. He said it ' was no use, as he was going to report or had reported it to the chief con- stable himself. Witness told him that it was a very unusual thing to withdraw the men from the town in the way he had done. , It was his duty to have with- drawn them, if at all, through him as an inspector. He said he did not know it was necessary to consult wit- ness. Witness was somewhat aggrieved that the Super- intendent should have drafted the men out of town without letting him know —Mr. Lewis asked for the production of the report made by Superintendent Large to the chief constable.—Mr. Fletcher said the report was a verbal one.—O'Donnell said a copy of the report would he found in the letter book unless there was great informality.—Mr. Fletcher explained that there might be an entry about the report in the letter book, but it was not in his possession at that time.— Mr. Lewis: We gave you notice to produce it.— The Chairman : I daresay it will come later on.- 13y Mr. Beckett: The superintendent was in bed when witness left the town he believed.—By Mr. Lister: You must have been on very bad terms with the superintendent not to go to him instead of following the men to Halton P—Witness : I was not on bad terms with him, but he was with me.—Mr. Lewis :• Why is it that you term poachers" suspicious characters ?" Witness : Because it is not recognised that a constable is legally employed in watching for poachers off the high-way, so that he is said to be watching for suspicious characters. It had always been the custom for superintendents to instruct those under them to describe the class of duty as " watching for suspicious characters." He supposed they were told to do so by the chief constable. On the 17th September Constable Boyle reported that he had been off his heat to watch for poachers—suspicious characters. Witness kept the report and told the superintendent verbally about it.—By Mr. Fletcher : He was not reprimanded for going away the morning after the 24th. Ile did not know that the superinten- dent had been patrolling the streets of Runcorn by him- self. He considered it would have been exceeding his duty to bring the men back from Halton. It was not exceeding his duty to go there. He did not stop to ascer- tain whether the superintendent was in before going to Norton.—Mr. Lister: Supposing the super- intendent had been away, and you had had a similar report made to you, should you not have done the same thing ?—No, I should not have cleared the town.—The Chairman: The real substance of your complaint is that the town was clear P—Witness : Yes.—Then I want to know in the name of reason why you took yourself off if you complain of the town being empty ?—Mr. Lewis: That is not my complaint I have nothing to do With O'Donnell.—Mr. Fletcher: I should like to know how you have got that report of Boyle's. I see it is addressed to Superintendent Large? Witness; I told him about it. I gave an explanation from Royle, and my intention was to bring his report before the chief constable myself.—Mr. Lister: Then you intercepted the document ? The report came through me, and I men- tioned it to him.—Do you mean you had a discretion ? I

In answer to Mr. Browne he for . at he had worked for the company for 31 years, tioneghteen of which he had worked at the box in ques- D. 1,1.1. Ile went on duty.on the 20th of June, at 10.10 the .„„ Mr. Young gave witness the instructions as to • atsnet7al train. Mr. Young explained the printed in- right al°"8- Goulden, on the engine, said, "Are you as /qr. ,„ay, John ?" and witness replied, "Yes, as soon ettaine 19ung gets on." When Mr. Young got on the that a., witness was at the window. He turned to see lie ,ie Points were all right, and believed them to be so. Whag'....under the impression that he pulled the lever to the "Ir. Young was going from the door of the box iooite•hetongine. When Mr. Young was on the engine he ter rnake doubly sure and thought that the mat- right, The ' words used by witness subsequently

===== 0002601_18900705_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
were, " Good God, haven't I turned the points ?",. He also said, "Yes, it's all right," to the sergeant.—By Mr. Preston: It was his impression that he had turned the 'light engine nn to the proper line. The only instructions in regard to the Royal train were that he was to let no train or engine pass after a certain time; He and Goulden were always the best of friends.' He had never known the points to fly back. The Coroner, in addressing the jury On the evidence laid before them, said that he presumed that the jury would be satisfied that the deceased came by his death at the hospital at Warrington on the 23rd June, from injuries received in consequence of the light engine of which he was in charge coming into collision with the Holyhead expiess train. He also presumed that they would be agreed that the collision took place in conse- quence of the light engine running, by some means or other, on the down-line towards Warrington instead of on the up-line. If they were agreed on these points the only issue to determine was as to the conduct of the man John Sankey, the person in charge of the signal box. In other words, they had to say whether they tbought the death had been brought about by negligence on the part of Sankey, and if they did find that it had been so brought about, it was for them to determine what sort el negligence it was—whether it was censurable, or whether it was of such a grave character as to justify them in calling it culpable, and sending him for trial for manslaughter. Sankey was not an inexperienced signalman. He had been in the employ of the Com- pany for a great number of years, and during the whole of that time had borne a most exemplary character, and had carried out the duties in connec- tion with the office faithfully and well. But they knew that human nature was not infallible. Sometimes, notwithstanding all the care and skill that were brought to bear, something might happen that they were not aware of, and then it came in the character of an acci- dent. It was no doubt his duty, since he had had corn- , munication from James Goulden, to pull over a lever marked 29. His next operation, when the train had proceeded to its destination, would be to turn the lever back again. Mr. Young left the box, and Sankey said that he was under the impression that he pulled the lever so as to enable the light engine to go in the proper direction. As a matter of fact he was under a wrong impression and thelever was not turned. In this case there should have been four levers turned, and it was quite easy for a man turning round to look to think that there were four when there were only three. If they thought that Sankey emitted to do his duty, the ques- tion arose whether he was negligent in the omission. Was his omission one of negligence, or want of judgment, or Was it brought about by carelessness. If they thought Sankey honestly believed that he had turned the lever, he could not see how they could say there had been negligence. It was impossible for a man to go on year after year performing a duty without something hap- pening over which hehad no control—that might happen notwithstanding all the care and ability he brought to bear. If they thought Sankey did what he could, and that by some inadvertence the omission took place, it amounted to a misadventure, and they must relieve Sankey from blame in the matter. If they thought there was negligence, censurable or criminal, they must bring in a verdict accordingly. After a brief retirement the jury returned a verdict to the effect that their unanimous opinion was that the de- ceased died from injuries received in a collision inadver- tently brought about by John Sankey omitting to turn the points—in other words " Death from misadventure." The Coroner: That is a verdict with which I entirely COELCUT.

===== 0002601_18900705_art0099.txt | included=true | included =====
-THE CASE BEFORE THE WARRINGTON MAGISTRATES.

At two o'clock on Wednesday afternoon, after the ordinary business of the Warrington County Petty Ses- sional Court had been concluded, Superintendent Weir said that they would proceed with the case against John Sankey, the signalman. Mr. Browne said that he was rather surprised to hear the police say that they were going on with the case. Their worships (Messrs. P. Stubs and J. Tinsley) would perhaps know that on the previous day the Coroner's inquest was held, and the unanimous verdict of the jury was•that the death was one of misadventure, and no blame was attached to. Sankey. The Coroner Mr. Brighouse, entirely concurred with the verdict. He did not see any good in going on with the case, for there was no probability of getting any conviction from the grand jury. Superintendent Weir: The prisoner was placed in a certain position, and his duty was to do certain things which he faller to do, and his failure resulted in the death of a person. Mr. Browne Yes, but it must be a culpable negli- gence of such duty. The jury unanimously found that there was none. Mr. Stubs said that he believed that the police had no option in the matter but to go on, seeing that the man had been apprehended. Mr. Browne : Yes, but it is only a waste of time. Mr. Weir: Of course, the Court will understand that I have no object to serve beyond public justice. The Bench decided to proceed with the case, and see if a prima facie one was made out, and Sankey, who sur- rendered to his bail, was then formally charged with having caused the death of Goulden. The witnesses called at the inquiry on Tuesday re- peated their evidence, the only other witness called be- ing Dr. Ernest E. Bowden, house surgeon at the Warring- ton Infirmary, who stated that about two o'clock on the morning of the 21st June, deceased was admitted to that institution in an unconscious condition. He had a large bruise on the left thigh, the bone of which was fractured. There was also some brain injury, which he considered to be laceration of the brain. Goulden re- mained unconscious up to the time of his death, which took place on the morning of the 23rd ult. The cause of death was laceration of the brain and general shock. In his opinion the injuries would be caused by a violent collision. • Mr. Browne, addeessing the magistrates for the defence, asked them to discharge his client. He com- mented on the finding of the Coroner's jury, arrived at after an exhaustive investigation, at which plans were submitted and inspected. The police were not serious in the prosecution, or they would have produced plans, as was customary. The act of Sankey was simply one of omission. He had been 31 years in the employ of the Company, who reposed every confidence in him. Mr. Weir said that it was not customary, to produce plans at preliminary Inquiries. The Chairman said that after carefully considering the case, they dismissed the charye against Sankey. The police could not under the circumstances have with- drawn the charge, but were bound to bring it before the Bench.

THREATENED CRISIS IN THE TELL-

GRAPH SERVICE. . The Central News says a serious crisis in the telegrayh service is threatened, the clerks at the chief office having , decided that they will no longer work overtime. The Office is already undermanned, and the average number of hours of overtime per week is from 700 to 900. A general refusal to work overtime will, therefore, very seriously affect the despatch of public business, and will in any case throw the department into confusion by the admission of the Postmaster-General himself in Parlia- ment that the working of overtime is not compulsory, and that the clerks are acting within their legalrights in refusing to perform it. Their contention is that, while they vainly ask for the redress of just and admitted grievances, the authorities have no cause for complaint if they decline to go out of their way to perform duties not imposed upon them by law or by the condition of their service.

===== 0002601_18900705_art0100.txt | included=true | included =====
FLOODING OF A WELSH MINE.

NARROW ESCAPE OF COLLIERS,

The Welsh newspapers report an alarming accident at the Dinas Steam Coal Collieries, Rhondda Valley, vhere four hundred miners have experienced miraculous escapes from drowning. It appears that for a period of twenty years water has been accumulating in an aban- doned shaft adjoining the Dinas Colliery, and while t he miners were at work in the four, six, and ninefeet seams there was a sudden rush of water from the dead work- ings. The volume increased in force and came rushing along the workings like a torrent. An alarm was raised throughout the colliery, and the miners, numbering about four hundred, hurried wildly from their work. Intense excitement prevailed Among the men, and it was feared at one time that large numbers would be engulfed. Fortunately, however, the imperilled miners escaped along the higher workings and reached the pit mouth in safety. Great anxiety was felt by the officials at the sur- face, and every endeavour was made to rescue the men, who were speedily drawn to the surface. Work is sus- pended until all danger is at an end.

===== 0002601_18900705_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FATAL ACCIDENT AT DALLAII

---- FORGE. On Wednesday afternoon Mr. W. T. Husband, Ideputy-coroner for South-west Lancashire, held an inquiry at the Bridewell Parade-room, touching the death of John Robinson (55), of 15, Wright-st., Howley, where he resided with a married daughter. The evidence adduced showed that the deceased was working at Hallam Forge on Tuesday morning, when he was crushed by an iron bell, weighing 3 tons 7 cwt., which was in course of being finished. One of the witnesses stated that he was on a ladder, fastening a chain to the inner side of the bell, and the deceased was working under the bell. Witness thought deceased must have pulled out some pack- ing, which caused the bell to topple over and crush him against some columns. He Was liberated as soon as possible, and a messenger was despatched for Dr. Adams, but on his arrival he pronounced that life was extinct. The Coroner thought that the jury could come to no other conclusion than that the death was caused by an accident, and, the jury- men being of the same opinion, returned a verdict to that effectiv Mr. T. Morris and Mr. Atherton attended the fhquiry on behalf of the proprietors of the works, and said that some provision would be made for the family of the deceased.

THE PENXY POST JUBILEE.

SOCIAL MEETING AT WARRINGTON POST OFFICE.

In connection with the Jubilee of the Penny Post " social" was held, on Wednesday evening, in the dining rooms of the Head Post Office, Warrington, about seventy persons being present. In the absence of the Postmaster, (Mr. Kerry). who was away at the Conversazione at South Kensington, the Chief Clerk (Mr. Broadhurst) presided, and in open- ing the proceedings referred to the object of the gather- ing. The Post Office, he said, had become a most im- portant element in the commercial and social world, and the 50th anniversary of the Penny Post which had caused such a developement of general business should scarcely be allowed to pass unnoticed, more especially as Jubilees -were the order of the day, and it was a matter for con- gratulation that the question had been taken up in such a spirited way at head-quarters. He was sure they all appreciated the endeavours which were being made in the interests of the widows and orphans. (Hear, hear.) No doubt there were some hundreds of cases of distress which the Rowland Hill Fund had been unable to reach and he thought their superiors had acted wisely-intaking • that opportunity of augmenting such a benevolent fund. They had not arranged any programme for that night, but they relied on all coming forward to help them with a song or something that would keep the mill going. The indefatigable Mr. Skelton was on the scene as they would see with his magic lantern. Mr. Ford had kindly undertaken to play for them, and Messrs Bennion and ! Whitfield had taken notthe least important duty in cater- ing for them. During the evening songs:were contribu- ted by Miss Gorton, and Messrs. Broadhurst (2), Jolley (3), Skelton, Leach, Bibby, and Bradshaw, the last-named causing great amusement, especially amongst the youngsters. The magic lantern was again a success, several new slides having been added, including well- known Warrington celebrities, and some good local views. At five minutes to ten an adjournment was made to the sorting-room where at a given telegraphic signal cheers were called for and heartily given for the Queen, the Postmaster, Mr. Broadhurst, and all those who had assisted in getting up the entertainment. An effort was then made by the aid of the electric light to photograph the group, but whether successful or not remains to be seen. A very pleasant evening was brought to a close by the singing of the National Anthem at 10-45 p.m.

THE LIBERAL PARTY IN WARRINGTON:

Sir,—You deserve the thanks of every Liberal in War- rington for your leader in last Saturday's "Examiner." It is high time the leaders of our party here bestirred themselves, for we know not how soon a general election may be upon us. Why cannot the annual ward meet- ings be held, and the committees elected to do their proper work. We have not yet filled the vacancy of President caused by the death of Mr. McMinnies. There is only one name to be suggested as our local leader. I refer to our friend Mr. John Crosfield. Let us ask him by a unanimous vote to become our President, and with him as our leader and with plenty of enthusiasm in our ranks, stimulated by the glorious victory at Barrow, and with a good candi- date, we shall certainly win Warrington for Liberalism.— Yours truly, Warrington, July 3rd.

A LIBERAL WORKER.

WARRINGTON'S OPPORTUNITY. Sir,—Mr. Caine's recent interview with a Press repre- sentative, practically amounts to an invitation to the Liberal executive of a borough like ours, to aparoach him with regard to his candidature in the Home Rule and temperance cause. His past services to the Liberal party, coupled with his expressed willingness to co-oper- ate with the party in the future in its Irish legislation, leaves it the bounden duty of the Liberal leaders to see that the proper steps are taken to make his conver- sion complete and to reinstate him once more in that party which it is evident he is sorry to have ever deserted. It is with this idea in view that I venture to suggest to the leaders of the party in Warrington, the opportunity they now have of securing perhaps the strongest candid- ate that could possibly be found to battle for us.—Yours truly,

W. KNOWLES.

St. Mary-st., July 4th. WARRINGTON BANDS. Sir,—l shall feel pleased if you will allow me a short space in your valuable paper to say a few words on the above. I was surprised to see so many bands taking part in the processions on Walking Day and wondered where they had sprung from. The people of our town should be proud to have so many good bands in their midst, and I am sure would appreciate their music more if the bands would turn out oftener and not confine themselves to the practice room. The music rcndered by the various bands was very creditable and I wonder they do not appear more in the park. The wonderful improvement in the League of the Cross band is very marked and reflects great credit on their teacher. The discipline and order in this band—which are the first marks of a good band—were much commented upon, and I have no doubt, under their able leader, they will yet makea name. But they must come out oftener. The style of marching of that band is worth imitation on Walking Day, for it prevents running and breaks in a children's procession. The Park Musical Committee complain that they do not receive sufficient subscriptions. I think this is the fault of the bands not taking the matter up and helping this deserving committee. I think a meeting of secretaries and leaders, having in view the formation of an association would be a great benefit, and if the committee would promote a contest, at the end of the season, of all local bands, in the Drill Hall, proceeds to be in aid of band fund, they would be supported by the public. -Hoping seme one more qualified will take the matter up. —I am, yours truly,

THE VACANCY IN MID-DURHAM.

At a special meeting of the Durham Miners' Federation Board at Durham, on Thursday, Mr. John Wilson, ex- MP. for Houghton-le-Spring Division, was nominnted the miners' vandidate, in room of the late Mr. Crawford. It is not expected that there will be any opposition, owing to the strength of the mining vote in the consti- tuency. Mr. John Wilson, Liberal condidate for Mid-Durham, issued his address on Friday morning. He states his opinions on the Irish question are similar tc Mr. Glad- stone's and he promises to address district meetings as soon as possible. At a meeting of the Liberal Unionists at Durham on Friday afternoon it was decided to contest Mid Dur- ham, but no candidate was selected. A. FAMILY POISONED.

4 lamentable affair is rep•rted from Dublin. On I Monday evening the wife and five daughters of Mr. James O'Connor, a Dublin journalist, on the staff of "United Ireland," after eating mussels which had been gathered near their home at Seapoint, were taken vio- lently ill, and manifested symptoms of poisoning. Medi- cal assistance was obtained, but within a few hours Mrs. O'Connor and four of her children were dead. The fifth daughter, though very ill for a time rallied, and is , expected to recover. The funeral of the victims took place at Glasnevin Cemetery, on Thursday morning, and was a most affecting spectacle. The cortege went by road from Blackrock into the city, and all along the route, which extended for several miles, many thou- sands of people assembled as a mark of respect to the bereaved husband and father. The attendance included the Lord Mayor and manv leading men and the journal- ists of Dublin. The city flag floated at half-mast. There was a great crush of people at the cemetery, thousands awaiting the arrival of the funeral. A touching inci- dent occurred in Lower Mount-st., where the coal porters on strike followed in a body, and as the funeral passed their band played the Dead March in" Saul."

PROMOTION OF A VOLUNTEREL—Thn following com- mission has just been issued from the War Office :--3rd Volunteer Battalion Cheshire Regiment, Second Lieuten- ant J. E. G. Groves to be lieutenant. SWINE FP:VER.—A case of swine fever was reported to the police,on Wednesday,as having occurred at Mr. J. H. Peter's farm, Burford-lane, Lymm. This is the second case in the Lymm police division lately, one having been reported from Partington last week. A FAREWELL TEA.—On Tuesday evening the members of the congregation worshipping at Sion Chapel, Cherry. lane, invited Mrs. Burgess and Mrs. Cook, her daughter, to a farewell social tea on the occasion of their removal to Liverpool. Mrs. Burgesss has lived upwards of 45 years in the house she is now leaving at Lymm Bridge, during which time she has been a member of the above chapel.

SUCCESSFUL MARKSMEN.—At the annual shooting of the 3rd V.8.C.R., held on the Owley Wood Range, Weav- erham, on Thursday, the following Lymn Volunteers were successful in the competition for the colonel's cup : —Sergeant T. Broadley, 4th with 77, £3 ; Sergeant J. Rogerson, 6th with 76, £2; Sergeant W. Kirk. 13th with 73, 10s. ; Sergeant Bmadley also won £1 in the " drill " with the score of 40. Sergeant-instructor Callighan won £1 in the sergeant-instructor Competition. NEWFIELD MISSION.—The annual meeting in connec- tion with the Newfield Mission was held in the Lymm Institute, on Monday evening. There was a very large attendance, the Hon. and Rev. R. Grimston (clerical , superintendent of the missionaries of the Ship Canal) presiding. Amongst those present were Messrs. J. W. McGill, L. P. Nott, Richard Dutton. and the Revs. Charles Clay and William Evans. The band of the mission from Manchester was also in attendance and dis- coursed music at intervals. Speeches were delivered by Messrs. Dutton, Nott, and McGill, and the Rev. William Evans. Mrs. McGill, who has allied herself very closely with the mission, or who is, more strictly speaking, the founder of the mission, read a lengthy report of the good work which has been done since it was inaugurated. In the course of her remarks she referred to the invalu- able help she had received from Mrs. Carmichael. A most successful meeting terminated in the customary manner.

LOCAL BOARD MEETING.

The monthly meeting of this Board took place on Thursday night. Mr. Cameron presided, and there were also present Messrs. Dutton, llarrison, Rogers, Brazen- dale, Swallow, ' and Thomason • Mr. Ridgway (law clerk), Mr. Mullard (clerk), Mr. Mort (surveyor), and Mr. Severs (gas manager). THE BOARD'S OFFICES.—The minutes of a special meeting. in which it was decided to ask Mr. Battersby to sell the Board the offices in which they meet, also the cottage adjoining and the land attached thereto, were confirmed. THE POLLUTION or MASSEY Bnoox.—The Clerk stated thatin accordance with instructions he had written to Messrs. Gibson and Co., informing them that the Board had had complaints of the pollution of Massey Brook, to which he had received no answer. He had also written to Messrs. Bower and Co. to the same effect, and they replied that they were doing all in their i:ower to prevent the pollution of the brook.—A letter was read from Mr. Colin Mclver complaining of the polluted condition of , Massey Brook, and stating its condition to be very bad. Another letter was read from Mr. Peter Rowlinson, of Statham, complaining of the pollution of Thelwall Brook.—Mr. Dutton said the brook was really worse since the complaints last month to the Board. Until the rains came it was something dreadful.—Mr. Rogers said from his knowledge the complaints were not at all exaggerated.—Dr. Fox asked if either of the two gentlemen could inform him where the pollution arose. —Neither gentlemen was able to give this information. THE DOCTOR'S REPORT AS TO MASSEY BROOK.—Dr.Fox, referring in his report to Massey Brook, said the smells' had been much worse during the last few months. He submitted to the Board two samples of water taken from the brook on the 19th ult., one above the worksof Messrs. Gibson and the other below. The sample of the lower brook was the clearer of the two, but had more intensity of odour, and he had no hesitation in stating it to be poisonous •, he was under the impression, from a has analysis, that the upper water was also poisonous. Ile pointed out that the Board was the only party allowed by the Rivers Pollutions Act to undertake legal proceedings; though during the inaction of the Board it was compe- tent for anyone injured to put himself in communication with the Local Government Board, who might, on the strength of such individual report or representation hold an inquiry, and direct the local authority to institute pro- ceedings, or not to do so, as tho result of the same. He pronounced the injury to the farmers as of the gravest character, and calling loudly for determined inter- vention of the Local Authority. Mr. Dutton approved the report, and said it was very hard on the farmer whose cows had no other water todrink.— Dr. Fox said that although the Board might take pro- ceedings against the pollution of the stream at once, in cases where the legal proceedings would affect trade it was necessary to get the sanction of the Local Govern- ment Board before any action could be instituted. He further said that the Board could never have the brook kept clean and allow certain works to exist.—Mr. Cameron said Mr. Bower asserted to him that he had kept and carried out the letter of the agreemeut he made with the Board last year, and the man in charge of the filtration bore out that statement.—Mr. Dutton proposed that a letter be written to the Local Government Board asking for an inquiry into the condition of Massey Brook. —Mr. Rogers,in seconding the resolution,said it had been a burning question long enough, and their present action would settle it for good.—The resolution was adopted. THE MAINTENANCE OF MAIN ROADS.—A letter was read from Mr. Bull, the county surveyor, stating that the Main Roads Committee ot the County Council authorised the payment to the Board of the sums of £174 16s. 6d. for the maintenance of the main roads within the district of the Authority, and £22 18s. for improvements.—The Law Clerk said the Board offered to maintain the roads for £2.33 as., and the committee had taken off about a fourth. The full amount for improvements had been allewed.—A discussion followed in which the members unanimously declined to entertain the proposal of the Main Roads' Committee, and it was decided that the surveyor go to see the County Surveyor and lay the matter before him.—The Chairman said it was likely that other Authorities might make a rule of asking more than was requisite for maintenance, and the Committee had served them alike all round, but that Board only charged the exact sum out of pocket.—A letter was read from the Town .Clerk of Crewe stating that the Main Roads' Committee had cut down their estimate for main- tenance to three-quarters the amount they had asked, and their estimate for improvements to one-half. They asked if the Board would send a deputation to Crewe on the 15th inst. to meet representatives from other autho- rities, and discuss the question with a view of submitting their case to the committee, which met on the 17th inst. It was resolved that Mr. Dutton and the Survepr be a deputation to the meeting. THE Ws Woms.—The New Manager reported he would require five new retorts at once, and the remain- ing five would require renewing at an early date.—Mr. Brazendale said it was not long since they were told that there was no one like the late gas manager, and now they had a report like that.—The Chairman said it was very disappointing to have a report such as had been read, after the one they had from the late manager at the last meeting.—Orders were given for five new I 0 torts. THE OBSTRUCTIONS AT THE CROSS.—The Law Clerk said in accordance with instructions from the Board at its last meeting, he had taken counsel's opinion on the obstructions round the Cross, and the advice was that the Board ought to succeed in sustaining a prosecution. It was resolved to take further proceedings. This was all the businasi.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JULY 5, 1890

THE PLEURO-PNEUMONIA OUTBREAK.

MORE CATTLE SLAUGHTERED. There has been no further outbreak of pleuro- pneumonia reported in Warrington during the week, but preventive measures are still being taken in the country districts around Warrington, and, we under- stand that a considerable number of valuable cattle belonging to Mr. Forster, at Woolston Hall farm. were to be slaughtered yesterday. THE QUESTION OF COMPENSATION. At the meeting of the Warrington Rural Sanitary Authority, held on Friday, Mr. J. C. Sutton. a member of the Lancashire County Council, referred to the question of compen- sation for the slaughter of swine and other animals owing to disease. He said, with regard to cattle that had come under his notice, and which. though perhaps 'la actually slaughtered in his division, had come from it, he could say that, so far , as he could learn, there was not the least blame to be attached to the person from whose farm they had come. He believed that with regard to certain diseases of animals it was sometimes absolutely im- possible to know that the animals were infected. Although the slaughtering of cattle was carried out to an extent which many people might think unne- cessary, he thought they must look to that proce- dure to eradicate the various kinds of diseases that were at present so troublesome, and by which so many farmers had suffLired.—Mr. W. HouGuToN thought it would be a good thing if a way could be seen to dealing more liberally with thole whose cattle were slaughtered. There was no doubt a fair valuation made of the cattle, but the greatest loss in a majority of cases was an indirect loss—the loss in a milk business, in pastures thrown useless for the rest of the season, and in produce for which there might be no market. Ile thought this subject was worth very serious consiueration.—Mr. SUTTON said that at the last meeting of the County Committee, there was:a strong feeling that one man was somewhat to blame, and some of the members went so far as to suggest that he should have no compensation. Ile (Mr. Sutton) opposed that view, and he urged that a man was doubly and trebly punished by the loss of his cattle in the ways Mr. Houghton had referred to. His sympathy had always been with the sufferer. But as to any extra allowances, beyond the value of the animals slaughtered, that was what the law would not per- mit.

THE CHESHIRE POLICE _ ADMINISTRATION. THE ADJOURNED INQUIRY AT CHESTER. LOCAL EVIDENCE. FROM OUR REPORTRR. Chester Castle, Friday. The Committee appointed to inquire into the charges brought by Mr. Slater Lewis, C.C., against the police administration in Cheshire, held its fifth sitting at Chester Castle to-day, the proceedings commencing at ten o'clock. Sir Horatio Lloyd, chairman of the Committee, again presided. Mr. Brasse,y, solicitor, Chester, appeared on behalf of Mr. Slater Lewis, who, however, examined the wit- nesses in person, and Mr. Fletcher, Northwich, again appeared for Colonel Hamersley, the chief constable of Cheshire. Before the proceedings commenced, Mr. James Tomlinson explained that I.is absence from the , sittings of the Committee had been caused by im- portant business engagements in London. The Chairman said that at the last sitting it was suggested that two cases of convictions of publicans had been omitted from the list supplied by Mr. Ashton, clerk to the 'Runcorn magistrates, and headed—" Return , of publicans and beerhouse keepers reported by ex-Inspector O'Donnell while stationed at Runcorn." Mr. Ashton now wrote the following communication.—" 3rclJuly,—As I stated in my letter of yesterday my books do not shew that ex-Inspector O'Donnell was concerned in the two cases you mention, but I have this morning seen the police constables who had to do with them. With respect to Phillips' case—the Friendship Inn —this man was on the 3rd September, 1888, con- victed for permitting drunkenness, and fined £1 and 198. 6d. costs, on the report of P.C. Brew, the wit- nesses being Gosling, Harrop, and O'Donnell. With reference to the suggested case of the George Hotel it was not the George Hotel, but the-Royal Oak,and the man was on the 13th July, 1888, convicted of selling to a drunken person and fined Li and 13s. 6d. costs on the evidence of Sergeant Woolley." Mr. Lewis intimated that Mr. Ashton could not know who reported the case to the superintendent in the first instance, and the Chairman promised that the fee books should be produced. The first witness called was ex-Sergeant Rowbothami formerly stationed at Macclesfield and other places in the county. He had, he said, received rewards of £1 for each poacher captured and convicted. The money had some- times been paid by Mr. Brocklehurst. He had also received money that had come from the late Mr. Legh, of Adlington Hall. Ex-Police Constable James Wright stated that formerly he was stationed at Graopenhall, near Warrington. When so stationed he watched and caught poachers, and he received from Sir Richard Brooke and Sir Gilbert Greenall rewards that reached him through Sergeant Bebbington. The sergeant received a five pound note from each of these gentlemen. It wasa reward for what they had done, and an encouragement to them in the future This happened is 1876, about Christmas, and the money was divided between five men. Witness re- ceived plenty of rabbits from the keepers; he had even had as many as seven couples at a time hanging up in the house. He did not think he was ever a week with- out game. The men used to receive word from the Chief Constable that certain gangs must be watched and caught. They used to watch at Grappenball two or three nights a week, the usual nights being Friday, Saturday, and Sun- day. They left their beats. but he had nothing to do with it except to obey orders. At Northwich also he had done a great deal of watching On one occasion he was one of a party of eleven watching at Platt's bridge for poachers, whom they caught. One policeman was left to watch the town and the out-lying districts. According to instructions they entered this duty as removing gipsies, watching suspicious persons.and extra journeys. He put in his journal" removing gipsies," by order of his super- ior officer, but he knew that it was a false entry. He was, however, forced to make it. Witness then gave further particulars of his duty in the Northwich district. The police caught seven poachers on private land at Grappenhall on one occasion. They did not know until afterwards that iVwas private land belonging to Sir R. Branke. There were forty of them altogether, including police, keepers, and helpers. The keepers took them on the land, but witness did not think that they knew they were doing wrong. By Mr. Fletcher Sergeant Marshall, Sergeant Nicholson, and others had told him to put "removing gipsies," when he was watching for poach- ers. It was the standing order, verbally given, and gen- erally understood, that they should never enter "watch- ing for poachers." Thomas Metcalfe, an ex-constable, recalled by Mr. Lewis, gave further evidence, and said that he had been as tar as ten miles from his beat watching for poachers. He could remember occasions when he and others watched for poachers night after night. Witness had received rewards in connection with watching and catching poachers-15s. from Sergeant Dodd, at Milling- ton, after the conviction of a poacher, was one reward.' Re had also received sums of 10s. and ss.—By the Com- mittee: No reward was paid unless a conviction followed ' —the general understanding was, no conviction no money. Witness also spoke in detail as to watching for poachers when stationed in various parts of the county. Witness had watched at Aldersey Hall. Before going on' duty they used to have a big can of beer from the butler, I which they drank from a horn. (Laughter. They I drank it in a stable. (Laughter.) They used to meet the keeper at the hall, and the butler brought the beer. On one occasion witness was regularly off his beat for a month, watching for poachers on the Duke of Westminster's estate. Asked" Do you seriously say you I were not on your beat for a month ?" -witness replied that he was never on his beat the whole of the time. He lay in the hedges on the watch, night after night. They had to book being on their beat during the dav,but as a matter of fact, they were not.—By the Committee: It was putting a lie down every day to make the entry. The sergeant ordered him to put the entries in his book, and the sergeant knew that the men were not on the beat. On one occasion witness was so ill with lying in the cold that he could not get home. The gamekeepers had rugs, and the police had bags filled with hay, in which they put their feet to keep them warm. There was no other provision. He remembered Colonel Hamers- ley examining the journals at Broxton, and he, once asked the meaning of the entry "suspicious character." The meaning was told him, and Colonel Flamersley re- plied " All right." / Sergeant Dodd. of the Cheshire police, said that he joined in May 1871, and was first stationed at Hyde, afterwards going to various districts. He was now stationed at Knutsford. When at Bollington hp had received rewards for the capture of poachers perhaps on two occasions, the last being some years ago. He had received money from Mr. Brocklehurst, but couldnot say who had told him to go to Mr. Brocklehurst. He had re- ceived, he should say, about £2 from Mr. Brocklehurst, but if another constable should be concerned in the case he would have a share of the money received in respectof such case.. Witness had not bad anything from the fund for a long time, but could not say that he knew absolutely that the fund had been stopped. Mr. Lewis questioned witness witness at considerable length as toinstauces of watch- ing for poachers at Knutsford. By Mr. Fletcher: He had never leftTthe highway when out on these expeditions. Poachers did not confine themselves to that class of offence, but were guilty also of fowl stealing and other offences. Sergeant Croghan, stationed at Birkenhead, and 16 years in the force in Cheshire. said that when at North- wich he had watched for poachers, but he had never known all the policemen taken from the town on that duty. He was sure that he had made entries in the journals whilst at Northwich as follows: Watching for suspicious characters and "poachers." Once when at Macclesfield he went to Mr. Brocklehurst and received Bs. from "the fund." £2 were divided amongst five el them, but witness did not report to the inspector that he had got the money. For anything he knew the fund might be in existence yet, but it was a standing order that no officer should receive money in the way of re- ward without the sanction of the Chief Constable. On the occasion in question witness acted on his own responsibility. By Mr. Fletcher: Most of the watching for poachers was done during the men's own time. Ex-Constable John Breeze, who joined the force in 1863 and 'left three years ago, said that he had twice been rewarded for catching poachers. Mr. Brockle- hurst, who rewarded him, was very, rich. He was pleased when poachers were caught—(laughter)—and was very good to the peliee. (Laughter.) It was well known that there was nothing vir- Any policeman now, and witness, who lived in Macclesfield, had heard three or four years ago that the fund was closed. Ex-Constable Eickford said that he had been twenty miles distance to watch for poachers, and had received railway fares and special duty allowance. Witness and some others went from Hyde to Macclesfield by order of Captain Arrowsmith. John Basnett, 1, Robert's-place, Castle-st., Chester, said that he joined the Cheshire police about thirteen years ago. He finally left the force on being "asked to resign." Be had watched, with others' for game on Lord de Tabley's estate, when stationed at gnutsford, both on the road and in the park. Witness did not go into the house, and did not have anything to eator drink. —By Mr. Fletcher : They patrolled the district before going to watch for poachers, but did not patrol it the usual number of times. Witness left the force in disgrace, having been convicted of an offence at Knutsford. The Chairman: There are no doubt several cafes alleged against this mAn. , Mr. Beckett: It does not follow that he is not telling the truth about this matter. Mr. Slater Lewis sit:id that Superintendent Egerton (now superannuated/ had been summoned to attend the inquiry but had not done so. His (Mr. Lewis's) charges were largely.based upon letters that had passed from Mr i Egerton'to Mr. O'Donnell. He thought that the Committee would only be doing justice to his case to peruse thpse letters, and then, if they liked, hand them tolVir.lietcher. They contained important evidence, and it would be neither doing justice to himself, to the or to O'Donnell, unless they were dealt with in

some form or other. He believed Mr. Egerton was not in a good state of health. s The Committee declined to admit the letters, as being ex-parte, and.said that they had no power to compel Mr. Egerton to attend. They believed it Would be preju- dicial to his health to do so. On the name of the next witness, Sergeant Costello, being called, the Chairman said they were glad to see that this was another case of a man having served thirteen years without a single complaint against him. Mr. Slater-Lawis observed that it was right that that should be mentioned, as it was not every man who had a clean sheet. Colonel Hamersley said that everyone in the Cheshire Constabulary was of good character; anyone not of good character would soon be out of it. Costello then gave evidence, which was in corrobora- tion of that of other witnesses, and which closed the case brought forward by Mr. Slater-Lewis. Mr. Fletcher then called William Bethall, recently retired from the force, and it was stated that there had been no complaint against him during a service of 27 years. fie gave evidence to the effect that on one occa- sion he'went with a number of men to Hooton Park. to wait for a number of poachers who were expected to sail film Widnes in a fishing smack. They did not come *ith that night's tide, but a short time afterwards eighteen or twenty of them came and the keepersattacked them. One of the keepers was shot. He had caught poachers fowl-stealing, and considered that in watching for poachers they were watching for thieves and murderers. Peter Smith, dental surgeon and chemist, Runcomr, denied that he and Superintendent Hollingsworth had ever been at Wilson's Hotel or the Royal Hotel after hours. He knew the Superintendent when the latter was at Run- corn, and living opposite him, met him constantly. As a result of his observation of the Superintendent, he should certainly not say he was a drunken man. On the whole Superintendent Hollingsworth was a sober man, though he never professed to be a temperance man. He was a very good superintendent, and a keen man for his business. Witness was present at a well-attended meet- ing held in Bethesda schoolroom this week at which a resolution (handed in) was passed. By Mr. Lewis: Witness believed Hollingsworth to be a sober, respectable, and exemplary character. He would not say that he had never seen him the worse for drink, but he had never seen him drunk. Witness did not think it out of place to hold a meeting in Runcorn when the matter was sub-judice. If the Committee had known half as much about Inspector O'Donnell as the people of Run- corn did they would never have listened so long to the inquiry. 0;Donnell did his work in a most beastly manner. (Laughter.) Witness once reported him to the superintendent for his obnoxious manner—to Superin- tendent Large. The superintendent did not report the matter further, because O'Donnell apologised to witness, by saying he did not wish him (witness) to do him any harm. It was during last summer. One afternoon O'Donnell walked into the Royal Hotel, without his hat, where witness and some other persons were present. They were all perfectly sober, none having had more than a drop of Scotch whiskey after dinner, and they were smoking cigars. The inspector told the manager it was time he turned the men out of the house,they had had enough. Witness went across to the superintendent and reported the matter to him, telling him that the inspector hail been most insulting. He (witness asked if they were to subjected to be such tyranny from a mean) devil like him, (laughter), or something to that effect. That was only one little example of the way in which the man had carried on. At this stage the inquiry was adjourned until morning.

CO-OPERATION. SHALL IT BE COMPULSORY OR VOLUNTARY? BY 11. WHITHAM.

The following letter, which we take from "The Pacific Union' has been forwarded to us by Mr: H. Whitham, of 810, Bay-st., San Francisco, and for- merly of Warrington : After reading the article in your last issue,headed "Compulsory Co-operation." I felt somewhat at, a loss, and repeated to myself the words of the first paragraph in the article, "that co-operation as a means of relieving the miseries of the working-men of Christendom is thus fare dead letter." Were these Words true? I asked myself; and if so, v(rhy were they true? If voluntary co-operation has so far failed, is corapulsary co-operation likely to succeed? I think not, and for the reason stated by Mr. Kinne in the article referred to—it would involve a "revision of the work of Almighty God in his construction of the human race." Are we likely to work fora com- pulsory State law, when we fake so indifferent an interest in voluntary effort to rid the world of all the sin and misery by which it is oppressed? I fear not; nay, I am perfectly sure that our duty lies in accepting the human race just as it is, and making every effort to fashion it to the required shape. But is it true that voluntary co-operation has thus far been a dead letter? I answer at once, and with sufficient proof at my elbow for such answer, "No; decidedly no." In England to-day co-operative societies ' abound in every town and city, and each year shows a rapid increase in theirnumbers. Not only are they increasing in numbers, but it is acknowledged throughout the length and breadth of the land that the members of such societies are the most orderly and the most sober citizens. The fact is, Mr. Editor, English workmen are quietly yet surely' I solving the problem of Capital versus Labor. As yet they have taken hold of but one feature in the very abstruse problem—that is, Distribution. Little by , little they are feeling their way Owards the other :---production. Capital., at present belongs to thel few; and under the present laws and usages of society the few are justified in defending and retaining it. We all know the almost innumerable 'isms by' which we are confronted, each one clamouring loudly for this capital, all putting the workingman , Well to the front, stating well his grievances—how 1 he is hatd worked, what long hours lie toils, and how small a portion of the spoils he gets for his share; but in all the meetings I have attended in I this city, of Nationalists, Socialists, etc., 1 have I failed to notice that the bona-fide workingman stands well to the front himself, prepared to state the means by which he peOposes to resume to him- self the capital, it is so kindly said, that belongs to him. t Now, Mr. Editor, I have had a pretty , close I acquaintance with" Horny-hand," and I can assure you he has a tolerably good sprinkling of conserva- tism running through his blood. He is honest, and, so far as I can see, he does not propose to rob either his employer or any one of those around him, but at times he gets a little bit unmanageable, and is apt , to kick over the traces. Well, pear with him, as 111 r. Kinne says, "his employer does not take him into his confidence, nor is he consulted in regard to his employer's business"; then how is lie likely to know that the business at which he finds employ- ment is not paying—when the employer tells him that the concern is simply running because it is less of a loss to run machinery than it would be to let it stand idle? I know that in scores of cases this has been done, for various reasons—the desire to keep, somehow, the trade connection together, to run the machinery, and last, but not alwaysleast, the desire to see the workmen have a wage to draw when wage day came round. how, my point of view is this: I think the em- ployer Is, and must be, as much dissatisfied with the present state of things as the employe that in voluntary co-operation lies the salvation of modern society. I would, if space permitted, deal alike with the question of science and improved machin- ery. I may do so in a further communication; but I would draw attention to the articles recently con- tributed by Mr. Burgen on the subject, and ask employer and 'employe, What is to be the end of it? Throunh competition, employers are at one an- other's throats; by every means within their reach ' they are compelled to produce ascheaply as possible: ',The means are not always—perhaps I should say are very seldom—acceptable to their workmen; hence we have continued strife, which, to the better informed on both sides, is becoming simply unbearable. Now, is it not clear that in co-operation, aided by the voluntary effort of all parties, lies the pan- acea for all our ills? More, I ask this question : Suppose the Nationalists and Socialists should dis- possess the present holders of all the capital now circulating, what would they do with all the poor humanity thus left stranded high and dry, with nothing left to bless itself with but—brains? It must be that capital and brains go together, or else we should ,never have seen o ridiculous a sight as a great strike extending to fourteen weeks, involv- ing waste in labour, wages and what is called 'Capital. Is it quite true that all the money, wasted on the workmen's side of the question, could not have been put to practical use because they were short of what Mr. Kinne calls " the department of effort 1 If it is true, I ask at once 4 what becomes lof our boasted educational system? Does it fit us for nothing better than novel reading? I think it does, Mr. Editor. I think it has fitted us to take right hold of the whole machinery whereby we can run the industries of the world, and so make life worth 'living. In voluntary co-operation lies the secret.' In this we have the solvent for the number Of hours we shall work. In this, we see the remedy for the drink question, because in this the secret is, physical exhaustion. In this, also, we have the secret of the comity of nations, necause labor would

WARRINGTON RECREATIVE EVENING

SCHOOLS ASSOCIATION.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Recreative Evening Schools Association was held in a room at the Museum on Thursday evening. Mr. Thomas Morris presided, and there were also present the Rev. S. Wilkinson, the Rev. M. Stevenson (lion. sec.) and Messrs. C. S. Burgess, D. Buckton, McGregor, Madeley. and F. W. Monks. The annual report, read by the Rev. M. Stevenson, stated that the Committee were pleased to inform the Association that the past session had been marked by steady work. The following classes had been held : Wood-carving at St. James's school, Latchford, teacher Mr. Brimelow, attendance steady and class much appre- ciated; wood-carving at Cairo-st. school, teacher Mr. Brimelow, about 40 members ; cookery class at Cairo-St., teacher Miss Henderson, of the Liverpool School of Cookery, large attendance; book-keeping at Smith-st. school, teacher Mr. A. Bennett, attendance at one time nearly 100 ; science class at Heath-side school, teacher Mr. B. Davies, between 30 and 40 members; night school at Hamilton-st., teacher Miss Mulliner, attendance aver- aging nearly 100; and French class at the Museum, con- ducted by 'Mdlle. Blanchet. In giving particulars of the various classes, the report stated that 30 members of the book-keeping class sat for the examination of the Lan- cashire and Cheshire Institutes ; of these 24 passed, 6 in the second class and 18 in the third. Twenty- two members of the science class sat for the South Kensing- tr..n Examinations in applied mechanics, and there was only one failure. The night school had earned a grant of £4O. The Committee had reason to believe that in addition to the good work done by the classes, an im- petus had been given to the movement in Warrington. The movement seemed to be in a fair way to become firmly established, and to show the necessary sign of true vitality—growth. The statement of accounts showed a balance due to the treasurer of £3 19s. al. The report was adopted on the motion of the Rev. S. Wilkinson, seconded by Mr. McGregor.—The Chairman said that although there was a great cause for congratu- lation with regard to the classes, he thought they should do much better if they had rooms more central and more fitting such an association. It hampered the work to have to find rooms for the different classes. They also required more funds, which, he had no doubt, would be soon forthcoming when that fact was made known. The classes were a great assist- ance to the youths of Warrington, and helped them to obtain better and more remunerative situations.—lt was resolved that the treasurer be empowered to employ help in collecting subscriptions, if thought desirable.— The Secretary intimated his regret that other duties made it necessary that he should resign that office, and on the motion of Mr. C. S. Burgess, seconded by Mr. Monks, he was cordially thanked for his past services.— On the motion of Mr. Morris, seconded by Mr. Madeley, Mr. D. Buckton was elected secretary and treasurer.— 'File Vice-presidents and Committee were re-elected on the motion of Mr. Madeley, seconded by Mr. McGregor, the name of Mr. Stevenson being substituted for that of the late Mr. T. Jolley.—This was all the business.

WORKMEN'S EXCURSIONS FROM

WARRINGTON.

The annual excursions in connection with several of the leading works in the town took place on Saturday last, a day which, as succeeding Walking Day, was generally observed as a holiday. From an 1 early hour in the morning the railway stations were ' marked by considerable animation. Some thousands of persons left the town for North Wales, Blackpool, Southport, Lancaster, and other places, and on the whole, they seem to have been favoured with fine weather, a marked contrast to the heavy rainfall and thunder- storm experienced by those who remained at home in the forenoon. Reports of the excursions are appended : CROSFIELD'S SOAP WORKS. About 600 of the workpeople of Messrs. J. Crosfield and Sons, Bank Quay Soap Works, who were accom- panied by about 100 of the employes of Messrs. J. H. Fleming and Co., had their excursion to Rhyl and Llandudno. A special train left Bank Quay Station at seven o'clock, but an unfortunate delay occurred at the new station of Mickle Trafford, a few miles from Chester. Here it was found that the connecting rod of the engine had been broken, and a telegram was despatched to Chester for another locomotive. This arrived about an

hour later, and the was resumed. The new engine slowly pushed the train from the rear into Chester Station, where the disabled locomotive was removed, and a larger and more suitable one substituted. Rhyl was reached at 10.30, and Llandudno at about eleven. The weather at both places was fine, and the excur- sionists thoroughly enjoyed themselves. The return journey was commenced from Llandudno at 7 p.m., and Warrington was safely reached before ten o'clock.

===== 0002601_18900705_art0118.txt | included=true | included =====
WHITE .CROSS WIRE WORKS.

The excursionists in connection with the White Cross Wire Works trip to Blackpool, numbered about 1,160, and there were two special trains. The first train left Bank Quay Station at 8.43 a.m., and arrived at its des- tination at 8.50. The second contingent started at seven o'clock, and arrived at Blackpool at 9.30. In the morning there were several showers of rain, but in the forenoon the weather cleared up, and was all that could be desired. The first portion of the excursionists left for home at eight o'clock and arrived about 10 p.m., while the remainder started about B.3oand arrivedhome at 10.40 p.m. RYLANDS' BROS. WIREWORKs. The annual trip in connection with these works took place to Southport, and about 500 passengers journeyed by a special train which left the Central Station shortly after seven o'clock. On arriving at their destination the trippers visited the principal places of interest, the Botanical Gardens at Churchtown, where the coronation of the Rose Queen took place, being well patronized. The return journey was commenced at 830 and War- rington was safely reached at a quarter to ten. SANKEY WIRE MILLS. ' " •

The men employed at the above works, with their wives, families, and friends, spent the greater part of Saturday at Lancaster and Morecambe Bay. A special train left Bank Quay Station, Morecambe being reached about 8-40. The weather during the morning was showery, but for the remainder of the day it was fair, and the excursionists. who numbered between 700 to 800, thoroughly enjoyed the out.

A Sr. HELENS SOLICITOR BITTEN BY A Mn Doo. Mr. Ernest W. Swift, solicitor, son of Mr. Thomas Swift, barrister, Sefton Park, the brother of Mr. John Oakden Swift, solicitor, St. Helens, left for Paris, on Thursday to undergo the Pasteur treatment. On Tues- day Mr. Ernest Swift found a dog crouching in the garden at Ivanhoe, his father's residence, in Sefton Park, and when the animal was disturbed it behaved in a peculiar manner. Mr. Swift there- upon attempted to drive the dog out of the garden, when it jumped at him and bit the thumb and forefinger of his left hand. The blood flowed freely, but Mr. Swift had the thoughtfulness to suck the wound, and shortly afterwards it was cauterised by a chemist. The dog was afterwards shot by the police, and on being examined by Messrs. Welsby and Son, veterinary surgeons, and Dr. Barron, of Rodney-st., they reported it to be suffering from rabies. No ill effects are appre- hended, but as a precaution the family considered it ' advisable for Mt. Ernest Swift to place himself under the care of M. Pasteur.

LATEST NEWS.

, THE LEEDS STRIKE. The dispute between the Gas Workers and Leeds Corporation was finally settled on Friday afternoon the blackleg difficulty having been amicably arranged. AN ENGINE-DRIVER KILLED. At Crewe, on Friday, William Sewell, a North-western engine-driver, died from shocking internal injuries. He was found lying on the line near Crewe Station, and it is supposed he was caught between the buffers of some coaches that were shunted. AN ELOPEMENT SCANDAL. At the Queenstown Petty Sessions Court, on 'Friday, Francis Hunt and Mrs. Sarah Crawthorne, both belong- ing to Hull, and who were arrested on arriving at Queenstown on Thursday evening from Dublin on a charge of elopement and larcency of £BO, preferred against them by the husband of Mrs. Crawthorne, were brought up, and on the application of the district in- 'spector they were discharged, as Mr. Crawthorne refused to proseeute. . ' MR. GLADSTONE ON COMPENSATION TO PUBLICANS. Lord George Hamilton, in his recent Bristol address, quoted Mr. Gladstone's former utterances on compensa- tion to publicans. Mr. Gladstone has written stating that what he said in former years referred exclusively to the operation of local option in proscribing licences in the mass, and had not the smallest referenpe to any non- renewal to which holders might be legitimately liable under the principles of the present laws. EXCITING CHASE AND Ci...PTURE OF A BURGL &R. On Friday morning the Barnsley magistrates com- mitted James Noon alias Walter Brammer, Venetian blind maker. Manchester, for trial to the sessions on a charge of burglary at the Victoria Inn, Darfield, last week. The landlord was awakened by tire prisoner being in his bedroom. The prisoner, who fled, was captured in the country after an exciting chase .by the landlord. He is well-known to the police, having been previously convicted at Liverpool and elsewhere. He is wanted for burglary at Doncaster. COAL FAMINE IN DUBLIN. Owing to the great strike of coal porters in Dublin a coal famine prevails. Hundreds of tons lie in ships on the quays, but the latter cannot be unloaded fox want of hands, and some vessels have left with their cargoes. The strike is producing a very serious state of things, and a large amount of intimidation by the strikers is taking place. Manufactories and city houses are run- ning short of coal. One large mill is only kept going by burning wood, and will probably have to be closed and the hands paid off. Paupers are being sent to the quays to load carts with coals from the ships under the protec- tion of strong bodies of police. The Phcenix Brewery Company would have been obliged to close but that the great firm of Guinness and Company came to the rescue and provided them with a sufficient supply of coals for the present. The Dublin bakers are in serious difficulty as their weekly supplies are exhausted, and there is little prospect of getting further supplies. The female friends of the strikers are taking an active part in intimidating any men who start work for the coal merchants. On Thursday night a man in charge of a horse and cart was assaulted and his horse taken from him by a number of women. A number of strikers are in prison for as- saults on blacklegs, and several will be brought up in custody. On Friday the little coal that is selling retail is fetching enormous prices.

The Government could not support pretensions incompatible with fair claims of foreign nations. Black Rod sum- moned the House to the Lords where Royal assent was given to Suck River Drainage Act and other acts. Sir W. Hancounr appealed to the Government to re-consider their decision, and to take the Bill to a Committee Of the whole House by send- ing the Bill to the Grand Committee. No time would be saved,as the measure-would have to be again discussed' when it was returned to the House. Under these cir- cumstances the Government should fix an early day for committal of the Bill by the House as a whole.— Mr. OSBORN MORGAN agreed with the suggestion a Sir W. Harcourt but Mr. RITCHIE objected. He thought it would only take a few hoursto pass the Bill through the Grand Committee in a form that would prevent pro- longed discussion when it came to the House.—The dis- cussion was continued by Mr. Darling, Sir G. Trevelvan, Mr. Pickersgill, Mr. H. Mawson, Mr. Howard Vincent, Mr. Picton, Mr. T. H. Bolton, Mr. Atherley Jones, Mr. H. FOWLER said the opposition would use its best endeavors to get the Bill passed this session.—Mr. W. H. SMITH said the Government had adopted the course of sending the Bill to a grand committee solely to facilitate its pro- gress through the House, and to prevent its being delayed in committee by obstructive tactics. The motion to refer the bill to grand committee was agreed to. [LEFT SITTING.]

WARRINGTON RURAL SANITARY AUTHORITY.

The monthly meeting of this Authority was held at the Workhouse on Friday morning. The Rev. G. F. Roberts presided, and there were also present Messrs. W. Long, W. Houghton, Heaton, J. Hough- ton, Aul-land, Jolley, Kilshall ; S. Sutton (clerk), Dr. Sephton (medical officer), and W. Owen (sur- veyor.) MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT.—Dr. Sephton reported that during the past month there had been 53 births and 22 deaths. The death-rate was 16.2 per 1,000, as against MO the average of the last three months, and 22.6 the rate of the corresponding month of last year. NEW HonsEs.—The Surveyor reported that certificates had been issued to Mr. Henry Grice for houses at Pen- keth, and to Mr. W. Bennett for two houses at Padgate. THE POLLI7TION OF STREAMS, AND THE EXTENSION OF TnE.Bonocron.—Mr. J. C. Sutton, clerk to the Board of Guardians, and member of the Lancashire County Council, referred to the question of the pollution of brooks and streams. He said united action was being taken by the Lancashire and Cheshire County Councils with a view to the purification of rivers, canals and streams, so that vrhathad hitherto been well-nigh im- practicable, if not impossible, would, in all probability, when these combined forces were brought to bear upon it, be successfully grappled with, He personally had been a considerable sufferer by the ,polluted state of Sankey Brook, the land along which was rendered utterly unproducti re in consequence of the poisonous waters. He was, therefore, so far as his own interests were concerned, likely to watch with some inter- est the steps taken by the new medical officer, and by the Committee who would act with him. Alluding to the proposed extension of the borough of Warrington he said the bills for the extension of Wigan and Oldham had failed and he saw the bill for the extension of Liverpool had also failed. These bills had cost very large sums of money, but he did not know how expenditure of that kind wasto be avoided. That authority was not materially opposed to the War- rington extension bill, but his attention had been called to the fact that there would, even after the extension was made, still be a portion of the present extra-muni- cipal part of Warrington left out. This portion had con- tributed towards the expense of providing sewers but as yet had received no benefit from them. The conse- quence of the extension would be that the authority's drainage district would cease, and the portion he re- ferred to would have no benefit from- the contributions they had been making for so many years. He believed that the sum they had contributed would be about £5OO, and he thought some arrangement should he made with regard to recouping those who would suffer as he had mentioned.

JOHN PENDLEBURY, DECEASED, Pursuant to the Act of Parliament 22nd and 23rd Victoria, Chapter 35. NOTICE is hereby given, that all creditors, and other persons having any claims or demands against the estate of JOHN PENDLEBURY, late of Bewsey-road, Warrington. in the County ot Lancaster, coal and builders merchant, deceased (who died on the 13th day:of April, 1890, and whose will was proved in the District Registry at LiVerpool, of the Probate Division of her Majesty's High Court of Justice, on the 27th day of June, 1890, by Frederic Pendlebury, of Warrington, aforesaid, coal merchant, and George Shawcross Pendlebury, of Oakley-st., Chelsea, the executors therein named), are hereby required to send the particulars, in writing, of their claims or demands to me, the undersigned, the solicitor for the said executors, on or before the 141 h day of August next, after which date the said executors will proceed to distribute the assets of the said deceased amongst the persons entitled thereto, having regard only to the claims and demands of which they shall then have had notice ; and they will not be liable for the assets of the said deceased, or any part thereof, so distributed to any person or persons of whose claims or demands they shall not then have had notice. Dated this 3rd day of July. 1890. ARTHUR BROWNE, Solicitor for the Executors. 1. Cloth Hall Yard, Warrington.
